Systems and Hardware

This appendix supplies reference models for the timing, memory, communication, and electrical limits that connect learned proposals to physical actuators. Its derivations and worked estimates help readers calculate response budgets, interpret hardware measurements, and implement the record formats used by the permission path. Each model states assumptions that must hold before its result can support a physical operating limit.

How to Use This Appendix

Consult this appendix when diagnosing physical systems bottlenecks, timing jitter, electrical rail instability, or hardware interface boundaries across chapters in this book.

When you encounter this systems symptom Consult For this quantitative tool
A real-time safety task misses periodic execution deadlines section 1.3 Rate-monotonic and EDF schedulability, Response Time Analysis (RTA), and Priority Ceiling Protocol
Neural policy inference shows non-deterministic execution times or long latency tails section 1.3 Execution-time analysis, static shape constraints, CUDA Graphs, and the limits of Extreme Value Theory (EVT) estimates
Real-time OS threads suffer latency spikes from page faults, cache bouncing, or IRQ preemption section 1.3 Memory locking (mlockall), stack prefaulting, 64-byte cache alignment, and top-half/threaded IRQ partitioning
A software deadlock or kernel panic freezes actuator control loops section 1.3 Windowed watchdog detection, rolling polynomial token checkpoints, and Safe Torque Off (STO) response
Inverter bridge MOSFETs burn out or trip overcurrent protections section 1.4 Microcontroller PWM complementary timers, dead-time insertion, Miller clamps, and 6th harmonic torque ripple
Heavy neural inference bursts induce compute brownout resets section 1.5 Inductive voltage droop (\(\ParasiticLoopInductance d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)), multi-tier decoupling hierarchies, and alpha-power gate delay
High-rate sensor DMA streams cause safety threads to miss deadlines section 1.6 DRAM bank-conflict penalties, row buffer thrashing, FR-FCFS starvation, and AXI QoS arbitration
Sensor fusion streams suffer temporal registration skew under motion section 1.7 IEEE 1588 / 802.1AS PTP hardware PHY timestamping, PI clock servos, and bounded clock conversion uncertainty
Shared memory queues between Linux and the safety microcontroller return torn or stale data section 1.8 Zero-copy IPC, POSIX shm, lockless ring buffers, triple-buffering, and CUDA IPC for multi-accelerator pipelines
Joint actuators fail to track sudden torque reversals, chatter, or overheat section 1.9 Stator \(\PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\) constants, lumped thermal ODE networks, reflected rotor inertia, and analytical jerk limits
Emergency braking spikes DC bus voltage and damages power silicon section 1.10 Regenerative capacitive energy surges, dynamic brake choppers, and bus decoupling sizing
Shared serial buses or Ethernet networks experience queuing delay and frame jitter section 1.11 CAN 2.0B vs CAN FD priority blocking, EtherCAT on-the-fly cycle budgets, TSN Time-Aware Shapers, and seqlocks
Monocular 3D bounding boxes distort and depth covariance diverges section 1.12 Pinhole intrinsic projection, Zhang’s calibration, and metric unprojection covariance
A mobile base breaches clearance buffers under surface wetness section 1.13 Kinematic stopping envelopes, information age lag breakdown, and velocity clamps
A calculation needs a value of the running machine, or a record needs its canonical name section 1.14 The machine data sheet with evidence categories and MLSysIM fields, and the register of the sixteen records
A record must be laid out for a fixed-size mailbox or log section 1.15 Zero-allocation layouts, natural alignment, proposal/record byte tables, and register field orders
A handover, fault trial, or release gate must be implemented step by step section 1.15 Authority-transfer, fault-injection, and release-gate protocols beside their records
You need rapid order-of-magnitude physical anchors across time, kinetics, thermal, and risk section 1.2 Rate hierarchy, blind travel conversions, thermal constants, and exposure walls

Numbers to Know in Physical AI

Physical AI requires quantitative intuition across computation, motion, and heat. The latency hierarchy popularized by Jeff Dean1 provides a useful starting point, but a delayed physical command also consumes clearance. The tables below separate representative rates and scenario calculations from bounds that require qualification on the actual machine.

1 Jeff Dean: Google Senior Fellow and systems architect. His latency numbers originally presented with Peter Norvig around 2010 established the canonical latency hierarchy across storage, memory, and network tiers (Scott 2012). Physical AI extends this hierarchy down into electromechanical actuators, stopping kinematics, and thermal time constants.

Scott, Colin. 2012. “Numbers Every Programmer Should Know by Year.”

The fastest events are transistor switching and local memory access. Operating-system dispatch and communication add scheduling and queueing delays. Actuator current then changes over an electrical time constant, while braking and heat accumulation unfold over longer intervals. These scales overlap; a task’s assigned deadline comes from its physical consequence, not from its location in this hierarchy.

Systems Perspective 1.1: Three physical numbers that matter most
  • Kinetic equivalence: \(1\text{ ms} = 1\text{ mm}\) (at \(1\text{ m/s}\)): At a nominal robotic speed of \(1\text{ m/s}\), every millisecond of perception, inference, or fieldbus latency translates into exactly \(1\text{ mm}\) of blind travel before retarding torque can be applied. At highway velocity (\(30\text{ m/s}\)), each millisecond is \(3\text{ cm}\). In physical systems, software latency is not waiting time; it is physical displacement.
  • Thermal divergence: \(\SiliconThermalTimeConstant \sim 1\text{ ms}\) vs. \(\ThermalTimeConstant \sim 100\text{ s}\): Semiconductor junctions heat adiabatically within milliseconds during deep neural network bursts, potentially triggering thermal throttling before heat spreaders react. Electrical rail droop is a separate response to changing current demand. In contrast, electromagnetic stator windings heat over tens to hundreds of seconds, tolerating transient overcurrent for aggressive maneuvers but accumulating thermal energy that eventually limits sustained torque.
  • The exposure wall: \(\FailureObservationInterval = 3 / \ConstantHazardRate\): Under a stationary Poisson model, zero observed failures gives an approximate one-sided 95 percent upper rate bound of \(3/\FailureObservationInterval\) after \(\FailureObservationInterval\) operational hours (Hanley and Lippman-Hand 1983). Very small target rates demand correspondingly large exposure. Runtime barriers can support a broader assurance argument, but neither a barrier nor a finite test proves the absence of failure.

Rate and latency hierarchy across machine levels

Control rates in physical AI rise steeply from the deliberative brain down to the physical body (table 1). Deliberative multimodal models (VLAs) operate at human reaction scales (\(1\text{--}5\text{ Hz}\)). Action chunking models emit future trajectory waypoints at \(10\text{--}50\text{ Hz}\). Beneath the proposal boundary, the permission path enforces safety barriers at \(1\text{ kHz}\). At the lowest levels, power electronic current loops and PWM switching operate at tens to hundreds of kilohertz to maintain electromagnetic flux and drive torque. For real-time timer schedulability and response-time analysis, see section 1.3; for inverter gate switching and dead-time modeling, see section 1.4.

Kinetic translation and safe stopping envelopes

Total stopping distance \(\StoppingDistance = \SpeedAtDetection \cdot \PrebrakeDelay + \SpeedAtDetection^2 / (2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\) combines the linear blind travel accrued during computational delay and the quadratic mechanical braking distance (table 2). As platform speed increases, absolute blind travel expands rapidly, consuming entire clearance margins before deceleration can begin. Section 1.13 derives the information-age and braking terms; section 1.9.3 relates joint jerk to electrical limits.

Edge memory bandwidth and action chunk amortization

For \(\Operations\) operations and \(\DataVolume\) bytes transferred from memory, an idealized execution-time lower bound is \(\max(\Operations/\PeakRate,\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\), where \(\PeakRate\) is peak arithmetic throughput and \(\Bandwidth\) is available memory bandwidth. The equivalent roofline throughput bound is \(\min(\PeakRate,(\Operations/\DataVolume)\Bandwidth)\). The ratio \(\Operations/\DataVolume\) is arithmetic intensity. This model identifies whether compute or memory transfer binds; synchronization, contention, and incomplete overlap increase actual time.

At small batch sizes, autoregressive generation can be limited by memory bandwidth. Table 3 divides weight storage by the assumed sustained bandwidth to obtain a streaming-only lower bound on execution time. A chunk decoder that produces all \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) waypoints in one weight pass can amortize that transfer; one that makes multiple denoising or token-generation passes cannot claim the same amortization. The resulting waypoint rate is an output-throughput ceiling, not a fresh-observation feedback rate. Section 1.6 explains how competing traffic reduces the bandwidth available to the decoder.

Thermal time constants across compute and electromechanics

For a constant heat input and a single thermal node initially at ambient, the temperature rise above \(\AmbientTemperature\) follows \(\JouleanTemperatureRise(\TimeInstant) = \SteadyStateTemperatureRise (1 - e^{-\TimeInstant/\ThermalTimeConstant})\) with steady-state rise \(\SteadyStateTemperatureRise\) and time constant \(\ThermalTimeConstant\) (table 4). Local silicon temperature can change rapidly during a computational burst, requiring thermal qualification of the workload and cooling path. Electrical droop has a separate current-transient timescale. In contrast, electromagnetic stator windings heat over minutes, allowing transient torque overdrive if bounded by an \(\MotorPhaseRmsCurrent^2 \TimeInstant\) thermal watchdog. For first-order thermal differential equations and stator winding degradation limits, see section 1.9; for regenerative energy absorption on DC power buses, see section 1.10.

Statistical safety bounds and exposure walls

For zero observed failures in stationary Poisson exposure, the one-sided 95 percent upper rate bound is approximately \(3/\FailureObservationInterval\), where \(\FailureObservationInterval\) is total operating time (Hanley and Lippman-Hand 1983). Table 5 shows the exposure needed to put that bound at three illustrative target rates. These are statistical calculations, not a mapping from a whole-system accident rate to a safety integrity level. In particular, ISO 26262 random-hardware-failure metrics do not describe every cause of a learned system’s failure (International Organization for Standardization 2018). Correlated trials, changing operating conditions, and unobserved failures invalidate a naive accumulation of hours.

Hanley, James A., and Abby Lippman-Hand. 1983. “If Nothing Goes Wrong, Is Everything All Right? Interpreting Zero Numerators.” JAMA 249 (13): 1743–45.
International Organization for Standardization. 2018. ISO 26262:2018: Road Vehicles, Functional Safety. International Organization for Standardization.

Fieldbus latencies, jitter, and determinism

Communication buses differ sharply in latency and jitter (table 6). Whereas distributed cluster fabrics (such as RoCEv2 or InfiniBand) prioritize aggregate bandwidth using credit-based flow control and statistical multiplexing, onboard physical AI buses must guarantee bounded delivery times. Industrial fieldbuses such as EtherCAT combine hardware forwarding with scheduled exchange and clock synchronization. A specific jitter claim requires the configured system’s evidence. Classical serial buses like Controller Area Network (CAN) 2.0B provide low-overhead bitwise arbitration for up to 8 bytes, but saturate rapidly under multi-joint control traffic. The Flexible Data-Rate variant, CAN FD, expands payloads to 64 bytes with accelerated data bit rates. Meanwhile, switched IEEE Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN) uses scheduled transmission gates to multiplex gigabit vision streams with hard real-time actuator commands. In contrast, non-deterministic best-effort Ethernet and shared wireless channels introduce tail latencies that exceed the reaction deadlines of high-speed electromechanical machines. For IEEE 1588 PTP hardware timestamping bounds, see section 1.7; for CAN 2.0B vs. CAN FD priority arbitration and EtherCAT cycle budgets, see section 1.11.

Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability

A real-time task is correct only if it computes the required result before its deadline. Average inference latency and throughput do not establish that property. A permission task needs a bound that includes its own execution, blocking on shared resources, and interference from other work. The following single-core scheduling models separate those contributions. Their guarantees apply only under their stated task assumptions; operating-system configuration and loaded measurements must establish whether the implementation satisfies them.

Real-time task schedulability under rate-monotonic and earliest-deadline-first scheduling

In contrast to general-purpose Linux time-sharing, real-time operating systems schedule tasks based on deterministic timing contracts rather than dynamic heuristics. A periodic real-time task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\) is characterized by its worst-case execution time (WCET) \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\), relative deadline \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i\), and period \(\TaskPeriod_i\). When multiple real-time tasks share a single core, schedulability depends on the priority assignment discipline:

Under Rate-Monotonic Scheduling (RMS), fixed-priority preemptive scheduling assigns priority inversely proportional to task period (\(\TaskPeriod_i < \TaskPeriod_j \implies \text{priority}(\RealTimeTaskIndex) > \text{priority}(\PairedTaskIndex)\)) (Liu and Layland 1973). For a set of \(\PeriodicTaskCount\) independent periodic tasks, with negligible scheduling overhead, no blocking or release jitter, and implicit deadlines (\(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i = \TaskPeriod_i\)), a sufficient condition for schedulability is the Liu and Layland utilization bound: \[ \TaskSetUtilization = \sum_{i=1}^\PeriodicTaskCount \frac{\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i}{\TaskPeriod_i} \le \PeriodicTaskCount \left( 2^{1/\PeriodicTaskCount} - 1 \right) \] As \(\PeriodicTaskCount \to \infty\), the bound converges monotonically to \(\lim_{n \to \infty} \PeriodicTaskCount(2^{1/\PeriodicTaskCount}-1) = \ln 2 \approx 0.693\) (69.3 percent). If total processor utilization does not exceed 69.3 percent, the task set is guaranteed to meet all deadlines under RMS. Task periods may instead form a harmonic set, in which every period is an exact integer multiple of every shorter period (\(\TaskPeriod_j = \HarmonicPeriodMultiple \cdot \TaskPeriod_i\) for integer \(\HarmonicPeriodMultiple \ge 1\), such as \(1\text{ ms}\), \(5\text{ ms}\), \(10\text{ ms}\), and \(100\text{ ms}\)). For a harmonic set, the utilization bound rises to 100 percent. In physical AI architectures, rate hierarchies are purposefully engineered with harmonic cadences to maximize processor utilization without sacrificing determinism.

Kopetz, Hermann. 2011. Real-Time Systems: Design Principles for Distributed Embedded Applications. 2nd ed. Real-Time Systems Series. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4419-8237-7.

When processor utilization exceeds the conservative Liu and Layland bound (\(\TaskSetUtilization > \ln 2\)), or when relative deadlines are constrained (\(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i \le \TaskPeriod_i\)), Response Time Analysis (RTA) evaluates exact schedulability from the worst-case response time \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i\) (Liu and Layland 1973; Kopetz 2011): \[ \WorstCaseResponseTime_i = \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i + \TaskBlockingTime_i + \TaskInterferenceTime_i \le \TaskRelativeDeadline_i \] where \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) is the maximum blocking duration induced by lower-priority tasks holding shared resources, and \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) is the interference from higher-priority tasks. The response time is computed via the fixed-point recurrence. The sum runs over the higher-priority task set. The ceiling counts how many releases of each such task can interfere during the current response-time estimate. An enlarged estimate may expose another release and therefore require another iteration: \[ \WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(\ResponseTimeIterationIndex+1)} = \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i + \TaskBlockingTime_i + \sum_{j \in \HigherPriorityTasks(i)} \left\lceil \frac{\WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(\ResponseTimeIterationIndex)}}{\TaskPeriod_j} \right\rceil \WorstCaseExecutionTime_j \] For fixed priorities, bounded blocking, no self-suspension, and zero release jitter, start with \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(0)} = \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i + \TaskBlockingTime_i\) and iterate until \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(\ResponseTimeIterationIndex+1)} = \WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(\ResponseTimeIterationIndex)} \le \TaskRelativeDeadline_i\) (establishing schedulability) or \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i^{(\ResponseTimeIterationIndex+1)} > \TaskRelativeDeadline_i\) (signaling deadline violation). See Comparing bus occupancy on CAN FD and EtherCAT and Response time analysis and execution variance for the role of response time variance in the nervous system.

In dynamic-priority systems, Earliest Deadline First (EDF) assigns highest priority to the active task with the closest absolute deadline \(\JobAbsoluteDeadline_{i,k} = \JobReleaseTime_{i,k} + \TaskRelativeDeadline_i\). For independent jobs on a single-core preemptive processor with no blocking and negligible scheduling overhead, EDF is optimal: if a task set is schedulable by any scheduling algorithm, it is schedulable by EDF (Liu and Layland 1973). For periodic tasks with \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i = \TaskPeriod_i\), EDF guarantees schedulability if and only if: \[ \sum_{i=1}^\PeriodicTaskCount \frac{\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i}{\TaskPeriod_i} \le 1 \] achieving 100 percent theoretical utilization without requiring harmonic periods. When relative deadlines are strictly less than periods (\(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i < \TaskPeriod_i\)), schedulability requires the processor demand criterion over the Demand Bound Function (DBF): \[ \text{dbf}(\SchedulingIntervalLength) = \sum_{i=1}^\PeriodicTaskCount \max\left( 0, \left\lfloor \frac{\SchedulingIntervalLength - \TaskRelativeDeadline_i}{\TaskPeriod_i} \right\rfloor + 1 \right) \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i \le \SchedulingIntervalLength, \quad \forall \SchedulingIntervalLength \ge 0 \] evaluating whether cumulative execution demand exceeds available physical time across all time intervals \(\SchedulingIntervalLength\). Under Linux, EDF is exposed via the SCHED_DEADLINE policy implementing the Constant Bandwidth Server (CBS) algorithm. A CBS task declares its runtime \(\CbsRuntimeBudget_i\), deadline \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i\), and period \(\TaskPeriod_i\) via sched_setattr(). If a task attempts to execute beyond its declared runtime \(\CbsRuntimeBudget_i\) within period \(\TaskPeriod_i\), the kernel dynamically throttles the task until its next period, protecting co-located real-time threads from execution overruns.

Reeves, Glenn E. 1997. What Really Happened on Mars? Electronic mail message, Jet Propulsion Laboratory.

In standard multi-threaded Linux, mutual exclusion primitives (such as futex-backed mutexes) assume threads will eventually yield the processor. When real-time tasks share data structures (such as seqlock mailboxes or actuator command queues) protected by naive mutual exclusion, they suffer from unbounded priority inversion (Sha et al. 1990; Reeves 1997). A low-priority task L acquires a lock; a high-priority task H becomes ready and blocks waiting for the lock; intermediate-priority tasks M that do not need the lock preempt task L, delaying task H for an arbitrary duration. To bound blocking time \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) in the RTA recurrence, real-time operating systems use formal synchronization protocols:

  1. Priority Inheritance Protocol (PIP): When task H blocks on a lock held by task L, the operating system temporarily elevates task L’s active priority to task H’s priority, preventing preemption by task M. Under PREEMPT_RT Linux, standard in-kernel spinlocks are converted to sleeping rt_mutex locks that enforce PIP automatically, while user-space threads configure PTHREAD_PRIO_INHERIT. While PIP eliminates unbounded inversion, it permits transitive blocking chains (\(\TaskBlockingTime_i \le \sum_{k} \WorstCaseExecutionTime_k\)) and potential deadlock.
  2. Priority Ceiling Protocol (PCP): Each lock is assigned a static priority ceiling equal to the highest priority of any task that may lock it. A task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\) can lock a resource only if its priority is strictly higher than the priority ceilings of all locks currently held by other tasks. PCP prevents mutual-exclusion deadlocks and guarantees that a task is blocked at most once by at most one lower-priority critical section (Sha et al. 1990): \[ \TaskBlockingTime_i = \max_{k \in \LowerPriorityTasks(i), \CriticalSectionIndex \in \TaskCriticalSections(k)} {\CriticalSectionWcet}_{k,\CriticalSectionIndex} \] where \(\LowerPriorityTasks(\RealTimeTaskIndex)\) is the set of tasks with priority lower than task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\), and \(\TaskCriticalSections(\BlockingTaskIndex)\) is the set of critical sections executed by task \(\BlockingTaskIndex\) with a priority ceiling at or above the priority of task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\).
Sha, Lui, Ragunathan Rajkumar, and John P. Lehoczky. 1990. “Priority Inheritance Protocols: An Approach to Real-Time Synchronization.” IEEE Transactions on Computers 39 (9): 1175–85.

Worst-case execution time analysis for physical ML pipelines

In classical embedded systems, the worst-case execution time \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) is determined via Static Timing Analysis (STA) (Hennessy and Patterson 2019). STA parses binary machine instructions, constructs a Control Flow Graph (CFG), and solves an Integer Linear Program via the Implicit Path Enumeration Technique (IPET) using cycle-accurate microarchitectural models of processor pipelines, branch predictors, and cache sets. In physical AI systems, evaluating \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) for learned vision-language-action (VLA) and diffusion policies presents three distinct architectural challenges:

  1. Microarchitectural opacity: Modern neural accelerators (GPUs, NPUs, Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) hardware) feature multi-level shared caches, dynamic frequency scaling (DVFS), out-of-order warp schedulers, and memory crossbar contention that make a sound static bound difficult without detailed timing and interference models.
  2. Algorithmic execution variance: Learned pipelines follow dynamic execution paths, including adaptive denoising loops in diffusion models, variable sequence lengths in autoregressive decoders, and data-dependent sparse attention kernels.
  3. Software runtime overhead: Deep learning runtimes (e.g., PyTorch, TensorRT, Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) Runtime) introduce non-deterministic host-to-device kernel launch queueing, unified memory migration, and internal memory allocation locks. Launch overhead and jitter depend on the runtime, driver, submission path, and load; a per-kernel constant is not portable across systems.

To constrain execution variability for neural policies that cross the proposal boundary, physical AI systems enforce deterministic execution contracts and hybrid measurement bounds:

  • Static shape contracts: Action chunk horizons (\(\ActionChunkHorizon\)), token context lengths, and sensor input tensors are locked to fixed maximum dimensions. Dynamic input padding replaces variable-length tensors, ensuring constant compute graph geometry and eliminating dynamic kernel recompilation.

  • Zero runtime allocation: All execution buffers, KV caches, scratchpad memory, and direct memory access (DMA) descriptors are preallocated during initialization. Dynamic memory calls (malloc, cudaMalloc, free) are strictly forbidden on cyclic control paths.

  • Reduced host dispatch overhead via CUDA Graphs: Fixing denoising iterations bounds one source of algorithmic work. Capturing and instantiating a reusable graph reduces repeated per-kernel host setup, as the CUDA Graphs documentation explains. Graph launch still uses the runtime and driver, and device scheduling, frequency changes, and shared-resource contention remain. A graph does not by itself provide deterministic execution.

  • Extreme Value Theory (EVT) tail bounds: Because finite empirical profiling cannot expose the true maximum execution time under worst-case hardware interference, engineers apply Extreme Value Theory (Coles 2001). Under the Pickands–Balkema–de Haan theorem, execution time exceedances over a high operational threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) converge asymptotically to a Generalized Pareto Distribution (GPD): \[ \GpdCumulativeDistribution(\ThresholdExcess) = 1 - \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter \ThresholdExcess}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter} \] on the support \(1+\TailShapeParameter \ThresholdExcess/\GpdScaleParameter>0\), with the exponential limit at \(\TailShapeParameter=0\). Here \(\ThresholdExcess = \TailRiskObservationLevel - \ExceedanceThreshold > 0\), \(\GpdScaleParameter > 0\) is the scale parameter, and \(\TailShapeParameter\) is the shape parameter (tail index). The shape parameter characterizes the tail behavior:

    • \(\TailShapeParameter < 0\) (Weibull domain): Bounded upper tail with a finite model endpoint \(\TailSupportUpperEndpoint = \ExceedanceThreshold - \GpdScaleParameter/\TailShapeParameter\), under the fitted model; an estimated finite endpoint is not proof of a hardware ceiling.
    • \(\TailShapeParameter = 0\) (Gumbel domain): Light, exponential tail.
    • \(\TailShapeParameter > 0\) (Fréchet domain): Heavy, polynomial tail. The fitted sign alone neither identifies a hardware failure mechanism nor determines whether a deadline can be defended.
Coles, Stuart. 2001. An Introduction to Statistical Modeling of Extreme Values. Springer.

For an acceptable target exceedance probability \(\TargetExceedanceProbability\) (such as \(10^{-6}\) or \(10^{-8}\) per invocation), a model-based execution-time quantile \(\ExceedanceReturnLevel(\TargetExceedanceProbability)\) is computed as: \[ \ExceedanceReturnLevel(\TargetExceedanceProbability) = \ExceedanceThreshold + \frac{\GpdScaleParameter}{\TailShapeParameter} \left[ \left( \frac{\ObservedCycleCount}{\ThresholdExceedanceCount} \TargetExceedanceProbability \right)^{-\TailShapeParameter} - 1 \right] \] where \(\ObservedCycleCount\) is total sample count and \(\ThresholdExceedanceCount\) is the count of observations exceeding threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\). For \(\TailShapeParameter=0\), the continuous limit is \(\ExceedanceThreshold-\GpdScaleParameter\ln(\ObservedCycleCount\TargetExceedanceProbability/\ThresholdExceedanceCount)\). These are fitted quantiles, conditional on the sampling regime, threshold choice, tail model, and parameter uncertainty. They do not certify a deterministic WCET. A placement record must distinguish measured maxima, probabilistic estimates, and justified execution bounds; the permission path must remain schedulable when the proposer exceeds its estimate.

Operating-system determinism through interrupt handling, memory locking, and cache locality

On a heterogeneous robotics system on chip (SoC), the nervous system and safety monitors execute under real-time operating systems (RTOS) or real-time Linux kernels (such as Linux patched with PREEMPT_RT). Interrupts and non-preemptible kernel critical sections can delay user-space tasks. A hard response bound requires bounding those intervals on the chosen kernel and hardware. In physical AI pipelines where control loops govern physical momentum, operating-system determinism requires eliminating non-preemptible kernel sections, bounding interrupt preemption latency, isolating execution cores, locking virtual memory, and enforcing cache locality.

Linux fair scheduling balances CPU service among runnable tasks. Its historical CFS implementation accounts for weighted execution time; newer kernels use EEVDF, which also considers eligibility and virtual deadlines. Neither policy establishes the physical deadline guarantee needed by the permission task. In physical AI systems, real-time control threads must instead be configured with POSIX static priority scheduling policies (SCHED_FIFO or SCHED_RR) across priority levels 1 to 99 (or SCHED_DEADLINE implementing Earliest Deadline First). Under SCHED_FIFO, the highest-priority runnable thread executes immediately and runs until it blocks on I/O, yields voluntarily, or is preempted by a strictly higher-priority real-time thread.

In mainline Linux kernels, disabling preemption during spinlock acquisition (preempt_disable()), executing non-preemptible softirqs, and servicing asynchronous workqueues can delay user-space execution by tens of milliseconds. When an unprivileged machine learning process or device driver holds an in-kernel spinlock, a high-priority 1 kHz real-time safety task cannot preempt the CPU, even if its timer has expired. A kernel configured with PREEMPT_RT reduces non-preemptible execution through four mechanisms, while hardware, drivers, and remaining critical sections still require timing qualification:

  1. Sleeping Spinlocks (rt_mutex): Standard in-kernel spinlocks (spinlock_t and rwlock_t) are replaced by sleeping mutexes backed by rt_mutex with Priority Inheritance Protocol (PIP) support. When a high-priority thread waits for an rt_mutex, the waiter sleeps and the lock owner inherits its priority until it can release the lock. Raw spinlocks remain non-preemptible; interrupt masking depends on the primitive used. Their maximum hold times must be included in the platform timing analysis.
  2. Preemptible Read-Copy Update (CONFIG_PREEMPT_RCU): RCU read-side critical sections (rcu_read_lock() / rcu_read_unlock()) become preemptible, preventing deferred memory reclamation from stalling real-time threads.
  3. Forced Interrupt Threading: Hardware interrupt service routines (ISRs) are split into minimal top-half handlers and prioritized kernel threads (irq/<irq>-<name>) scheduled under SCHED_FIFO.
  4. High-Resolution Timers (hrtimers): Traditional Linux timer ticks execute at coarse periodic jiffies (\(1\text{ ms}\) or \(4\text{ ms}\) granularity), introducing substantial quantization error and timer slack. PREEMPT_RT binds the timer subsystem to hardware per-CPU clock event devices (such as the ARM Generic Timer or x86 Local APIC), enabling sub-microsecond timer resolution. Real-time cyclic threads invoke clock_nanosleep(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, TIMER_ABSTIME, &next_period, NULL) using absolute monotonic deadlines, avoiding cumulative drift from relative sleeps. Wake-up jitter remains part of the response-time bound.

When an external hardware peripheral (such as an IMU, joint encoder counter, or fieldbus controller) asserts an interrupt request (IRQ), the processor halts user execution and saves register context. It then vectors to the hardware ISR via the interrupt controller (such as an ARM Generic Interrupt Controller GICv3 or x86 APIC). The worst-case interrupt latency \(\WorstCaseInterruptLatency\) to enter the hardware interrupt handler can be budgeted as: \[ \WorstCaseInterruptLatency = \LongestInterruptMaskTime + \PipelineFlushDelay + \ContextSaveTime + \sum_{j \in \HigherPriorityTasks(\text{irq})} {\IsrExecutionTime}_{j} \] This expression does not include threaded-handler work or the subsequent scheduling delay before a user-space task runs. Here \(\LongestInterruptMaskTime\) is the longest duration during which CPU interrupts were disabled across the kernel, \(\PipelineFlushDelay\) is the processor pipeline flush delay, \(\ContextSaveTime\) is the hardware register preservation time, and \(\sum {\IsrExecutionTime}_{j}\) represents the execution duration of higher-priority ISRs.

To prevent high-bandwidth sensor streams (such as GigE Vision cameras or 3D lidars streaming gigabits of packet traffic) from generating an interrupt storm that starves the 1 kHz permission path, real-time architectures strictly enforce top-half and bottom-half partitioning:

  • Top-half (Hardware ISR): Executes in hard-interrupt context with CPU interrupts masked. It executes the absolute minimum hardware operations: reading the peripheral interrupt status register, clearing the physical interrupt line, latching high-resolution hardware counter timestamps, and waking the associated threaded IRQ. The handler’s maximum execution time must be bounded on the target.
  • Bottom-half (Threaded IRQ): In PREEMPT_RT and RTOS environments, complex packet deserialization, frame CRC verification, and data copies are deferred to dedicated kernel threads (irq/<irq>-<name>). By configuring these threaded IRQs under SCHED_FIFO with priorities lower than the 1 kHz safety monitor (e.g., setting camera IRQ threads to priority 50 and the motor permission thread to priority 90), the scheduler prevents those lower-priority threads from preempting the control task. Hard-interrupt top halves and shared-memory traffic still contribute interference.

Heterogeneous multi-core robotics SoCs must also isolate deterministic control threads from background operating-system disturbances. Kernel boot parameters and memory layout rules establish strict CPU shielding and cache locality:

  • isolcpus=2,3: Removes cores 2 and 3 from the general kernel scheduler balancing domain, reducing automatic load balancing onto those cores; explicit affinity, cpusets, and kernel work still require configuration.
  • nohz_full=2,3: Enables full dynamic tickless operation. When an isolated core runs a single real-time thread, the kernel can suppress the periodic scheduling timer tick (\(100\text{--}1000\text{ Hz}\)), removing that source of periodic interruption while other interrupts and scheduling delays remain.
  • rcu_nocbs=2,3: Offloads RCU callback processing from isolated cores to housekeeping cores (cores 0 and 1).
  • IRQ Affinity Partitioning: Peripheral hardware interrupts are directed away from real-time cores by writing CPU bitmasks to /proc/irq/<id>/smp_affinity. High-rate camera MSI-X vectors and Ethernet controller interrupts are pinned exclusively to housekeeping cores, reducing controllable interrupt interference. Nonmaskable events, local timers, and platform firmware disturbances still require accounting.
  • Cache Line Alignment and False Sharing Elimination: Cache-line size is architecture dependent; the examples here assume 64-byte lines. When multiple CPU cores access independent variables that reside within the same 64-byte line, hardware cache coherency protocols (MESI/MOESI) repeatedly invalidate the line across core private caches upon every store, a phenomenon known as false sharing or cache line bouncing. A single false sharing interaction can stall a real-time core for 50 to 100 clock cycles. Separating independently written state into aligned, padded cache-line slots avoids false sharing; merely aligning the beginning of a larger structure does not isolate every member. The target’s remaining cache misses and contention still need timing bounds. Furthermore, real-time control paths pre-warm L1 instruction and data caches during initialization by executing a dummy pass over critical control tables before arming actuators.

Virtual memory subsystems rely on demand paging, so deterministic execution also requires locking virtual memory and eliminating page faults. When a process accesses an unmapped virtual address, the CPU raises an architectural page fault exception (#PF) and suspends execution. The kernel page-table handler then allocates a physical page frame, updates translation lookaside buffer (TLB) entries, and zeroes the memory (a minor page fault, requiring \(10\text{--}50\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)). If the requested page was swapped to secondary flash storage, the thread blocks on disk I/O (a major page fault, requiring \(10\text{--}50\text{ ms}\)). Furthermore, Transparent Huge Pages (THP) memory compaction (khugepaged) can asynchronously stall threads for tens of milliseconds while defragmenting physical memory pages. A page fault can consume the remaining response-time margin; its mere occurrence does not imply that every deadline is missed.

The following Linux/glibc sketch illustrates initialization and absolute-period sleeping. It assumes the shown page and cache-line sizes. Production code must also validate limits and privileges, measure completion against the deadline, and enter the assessed fallback on an overrun. A late absolute sleep can return immediately; it must not trigger an uncontrolled series of catch-up actuator writes.

// Real-time process initialization, memory locking, and cyclic execution
#define _GNU_SOURCE
#include <sys/mman.h>
#include <sched.h>
#include <pthread.h>
#include <malloc.h>
#include <time.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <errno.h>

#define REALTIME_PRIORITY   90
#define CONTROL_CPU_CORE    2
#define STACK_PREFAULT_SZ   (256 * 1024)       // 256 KB stack
#define HEAP_PREALLOC_SZ    (16 * 1024 * 1024) // 16 MB pre-allocated arena
#define PERIOD_NS           1000000ULL         // 1 ms period (1 kHz)

// 64-byte cache-aligned telemetry slot to prevent false sharing
typedef struct __attribute__((aligned(64))) {
    uint64_t sequence_id;
    uint64_t timestamp_ns;
    float joint_torques[8];
    uint32_t status_flags;
    uint8_t reserved[12];
} realtime_telemetry_t;

static realtime_telemetry_t g_telemetry;
static volatile uint8_t *g_realtime_heap_arena = NULL;

void configure_realtime_process(int target_cpu, int rt_priority, size_t stack_size) {
    // 1. Bind execution to dedicated isolated CPU core (isolcpus / nohz_full)
    cpu_set_t cpuset;
    CPU_ZERO(&cpuset);
    CPU_SET(target_cpu, &cpuset);
    if (sched_setaffinity(0, sizeof(cpu_set_t), &cpuset) != 0) {
        perror("sched_setaffinity failed");
        exit(EXIT_FAILURE);
    }

    // 2. Configure POSIX static real-time priority (SCHED_FIFO)
    struct sched_param sp = { .sched_priority = rt_priority };
    if (sched_setscheduler(0, SCHED_FIFO, &sp) != 0) {
        perror("sched_setscheduler SCHED_FIFO failed");
        exit(EXIT_FAILURE);
    }

    // 3. Lock all current and future virtual address mappings into physical RAM
    if (mlockall(MCL_CURRENT | MCL_FUTURE) != 0) {
        perror("mlockall failed");
        exit(EXIT_FAILURE);
    }

    // 4. Disable glibc dynamic heap trimming and anonymous mmap allocation
    mallopt(M_TRIM_THRESHOLD, -1);
    mallopt(M_MMAP_MAX, 0);

    // 5. Pre-allocate and prefault the dedicated heap arena
    g_realtime_heap_arena = (uint8_t *)malloc(HEAP_PREALLOC_SZ);
    if (!g_realtime_heap_arena) {
        perror("malloc heap arena failed");
        exit(EXIT_FAILURE);
    }
    // Touch every 4 KB page to force physical page frame allocation and TLB population
    for (size_t i = 0; i < HEAP_PREALLOC_SZ; i += 4096) {
        g_realtime_heap_arena[i] = 0;
    }

    // 6. Prefault the thread execution stack to its declared maximum depth
    volatile char stack_buffer[stack_size];
    for (size_t i = 0; i < stack_size; i += 4096) {
        stack_buffer[i] = 0; // Trigger minor page fault and populate TLB entry
    }
}

void run_periodic_control_loop(void) {
    struct timespec next_period;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &next_period);

    // A real implementation warms the actual control code and data before arming.

    while (1) {
        // Compute next absolute wake epoch (eliminates cumulative drift)
        next_period.tv_nsec += PERIOD_NS;
        while (next_period.tv_nsec >= 1000000000L) {
            next_period.tv_nsec -= 1000000000L;
            next_period.tv_sec  += 1;
        }

        // High-resolution absolute sleep
        int ret;
        do {
            ret = clock_nanosleep(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, TIMER_ABSTIME, &next_period, NULL);
        } while (ret == EINTR);
        if (ret != 0) {
            fprintf(stderr, "clock_nanosleep: %s\n", strerror(ret));
            break;
        }

        // ZERO-ALLOCATION CYCLIC CONTROL LOGIC
        // 1. Ingest sensor state from lock-free shared memory
        // 2. Evaluate Control Barrier Functions (CBFs)
        // 3. Dispatch admitted setpoints to EtherCAT master
        g_telemetry.sequence_id++;
    }
}

Beyond address space locking and stack prefaulting, the cyclic 1 kHz control loop operates under a strict zero dynamic allocation invariant. In general-purpose systems programming, developers routinely rely on dynamic memory managers (malloc, free, C++ new, delete) or standard container expansions (std::vector::push_back). In a real-time control loop, however, dynamic allocation introduces three catastrophic failure modes:

  1. Arena Mutex Contention: Modern allocators (such as ptmalloc in glibc) partition memory into multiple heap arenas protected by mutual-exclusion mutexes. If another thread in the same process acquires an arena lock, a high-priority 1 kHz real-time control thread calling malloc blocks on that mutex, suffering from priority inversion and waiting milliseconds.
  2. Kernel Syscall Invocations: When an allocator exhausts its active arena chunk, it issues brk() or anonymous mmap() system calls. These kernel entries acquire the process’s mmap_lock (or mmap_sem), which is shared with background kernel threads such as khugepaged performing transparent huge page compaction or memory defragmentation, stalling user-space execution for tens of milliseconds.
  3. Heap Fragmentation: Repeated cyclic allocations and deallocations fragment the heap, degrading allocation search times across segregated free lists into non-deterministic search loops.

Consequently, all message structures, matrix workspace buffers, trajectory buffers, and seqlock mailbox slots must be statically allocated at startup or drawn from fixed-size, lock-free memory pools (such as slab allocators). Furthermore, mlockall(MCL_FUTURE) must be paired with explicit memory pre-allocation and page prefaulting. Linux uses optimistic memory allocation (overcommit), so virtual address ranges returned by malloc() are not backed by physical page frames until the thread actually writes to each 4 KB page. Touching an uninitialized buffer for the first time inside the control loop triggers a minor page fault exception (#PF). The kernel page-fault handler must then allocate physical memory, zero the page, and update the MMU page table and TLB entries, a stall of \(10\text{--}50\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) that jeopardizes sub-millisecond control deadlines. Prefaulting and successful memory locking establish page residency before arming actuators. TLB and cache entries can still be evicted, so their miss costs remain in the execution budget; copy-on-write faults and later mapping changes must also be excluded.

Hardware watchdogs and fault response

In distributed computing clusters, node liveness and consensus are managed via software heartbeats and distributed leases (such as Raft heartbeats, ZooKeeper leases, or Kubernetes node probes) operating over second-scale intervals. If a worker node crashes, the cluster orchestrator reschedules containers onto alternative nodes. In physical AI systems, however, an autonomous machine traveling through physical space cannot wait seconds for a software orchestrator. If the GPU driver deadlocks, memory bus crossbars stall, or a kernel panic freezes the operating system, physical momentum continues unchecked. To detect a missed execution checkpoint, an embedded design uses a hardware watchdog timer. Its clock and response path must be independent of the monitored processor. An independent crystal oscillator or low-speed internal oscillator (LSI) completely decoupled from the main system phase-locked loop (PLL) is required.

The hardware watchdog timer comprises an autonomous down-counter register \(\WatchdogCounterReading(\TimeInstant)\) clocked at frequency \(\WatchdogClockFrequency\) (typically \(32\text{ kHz}\) from an independent on-chip RC oscillator). At system initialization, the register is loaded with a threshold value \(\WatchdogReloadCount\). Software execution threads must periodically refresh (or “kick”) the register before the count decrements to zero: \[ \WatchdogCounterReading(\TimeInstant) = \WatchdogReloadCount - \left\lfloor \WatchdogClockFrequency \cdot \TimeInstant \right\rfloor \] At expiry, the timer asserts its configured reset or fault output. A separately wired trip is required when processor reset alone is insufficient to de-energize the actuators.

In safety-critical systems, simple down-counters are vulnerable to runaway execution bugs: if a corrupted control loop enters an infinite spin-loop that happens to execute the watchdog refresh instruction, the watchdog is continually fed despite the control failure. To prevent this, robust physical AI architectures employ a Windowed Watchdog Timer (WWDG). A windowed watchdog defines both an upper counter threshold \(\WatchdogUpperWindowCount\) and a lower counter threshold \(\WatchdogLowerWindowCount\). The software refresh is valid only while the counter lies between them: \[ \WatchdogLowerWindowCount \le \WatchdogCounterReading(\TimeInstant_k) \le \WatchdogUpperWindowCount \] Because the counter falls at the fixed rate \(\WatchdogClockFrequency\), the two counter thresholds convert to the time bounds \(\WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\) and \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\) that The Boundary Contract uses for the service window, measured from the last reload: \[ \WatchdogEarliestServiceTime = \frac{\WatchdogReloadCount - \WatchdogUpperWindowCount}{\WatchdogClockFrequency}, \quad \WatchdogLatestServiceTime = \frac{\WatchdogReloadCount - \WatchdogLowerWindowCount}{\WatchdogClockFrequency} \] If software attempts to refresh the watchdog too early (\(\TimeInstant_k < \WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\), equivalently \(\WatchdogCounterReading(\TimeInstant_k) > \WatchdogUpperWindowCount\)), the early write indicates an abnormal loop short-circuit or clock glitch. Refreshing too early or failing to refresh before \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\) trips the hardware watchdog controller immediately.

Feeding the watchdog directly from a periodic timer interrupt is an unsafe anti-pattern, because the timer interrupt may continue firing even if the primary motion planning and safety monitor threads are deadlocked. To check completion of the expected software checkpoints, control software can implement multi-stage token-passing checkpointing using rolling polynomial accumulators: \[ \WatchdogToken_{k+1} = \text{CRC32}\left( \WatchdogToken_k \oplus \text{Signature}_k \right) \]

  1. At the beginning of the 1 kHz tick, a watchdog token \(\WatchdogToken_0\) is generated from the hardware monotonic cycle counter.
  2. Stage 1 (Sensor Ingress): Validates camera, IMU, and joint telemetry timestamps, updating the token: \(\WatchdogToken_1 = \text{CRC32}(\WatchdogToken_0 \oplus \text{sensor\_valid\_signature})\).
  3. Stage 2 (State Estimation & Tracking): Computes joint tracking errors, updating \(\WatchdogToken_2 = \text{CRC32}(\WatchdogToken_1 \oplus \text{tracking\_valid\_signature})\).
  4. Stage 3 (Permission Enforcer): Validates Control Barrier Functions and stopping distance envelopes, computing \(\WatchdogToken_3 = \text{CRC32}(\WatchdogToken_2 \oplus \text{enforce\_valid\_signature})\).
  5. Stage 4 (Actuator Dispatch): Transmits admitted setpoints across the fieldbus, computing \(\FinalWatchdogToken = \text{CRC32}(\WatchdogToken_3 \oplus \text{dispatch\_valid\_signature})\).

Only if \(\FinalWatchdogToken\) matches the expected value for this tick may the watchdog refresh routine service the timer. The token detects omitted or misordered checkpoints under its fault model; a CRC is neither authentication nor proof that the computed state or command is physically correct.

A watchdog timeout must reach a validated hardware fail-safe response path independently of the processor it monitors. A CPU reset or non-maskable interrupt alone does not establish that connection. When the assessed response includes safe torque off (STO), a separately wired output disables the gate drive that generates motor torque; the wiring, response time, and diagnostic coverage require device-specific qualification. Gate disable does not establish an instantaneous mechanical stop. Winding current takes time to decay, moving axes may coast, and a gravity-loaded axis may require a holding brake or a powered stop before torque removal. The complete watchdog-to-plant response belongs in the stopping budget.2

2 Hardware Watchdog Down-Counters: A configured watchdog expires when its expected service does not occur. Its oscillator, timeout, output wiring, and plant response differ by device. Feeding it merely from an unrelated interrupt does not prove that the monitored control computation progressed; a firmware-fed reset also need not signal a drive. See the Linux watchdog API for reset behavior and The Fallback Ladder for stop selection.

Inverter Timers and Power Electronics Gate Drive

In machine learning control frameworks (such as reinforcement learning policies, imitation learning, or model predictive control pipelines), actuator actions are formulated as continuous numerical setpoints, such as commanded joint torques \(\JointTorque^*\) or angular velocities \(\GeneralizedVelocities^*\). In physical realization, however, torque is generated by synthesizing rotating electromagnetic stator flux through high-frequency switching of power semiconductor bridges (typically at \(20\text{ kHz}\) to \(100\text{ kHz}\)). Actuator motor drives employ three-phase half-bridge inverters to convert DC bus voltage into alternating stator currents.

Microcontroller timer dead-time insertion

Bridging the digital software domain with physical power silicon requires strict microarchitectural timing interlocks. Each inverter phase comprises a half-bridge with a high-side power MOSFET (or GaN FET / IGBT) and a low-side power MOSFET connected across the DC supply rail \(\DcBusVoltage\). Microcontroller timer peripherals generate complementary pulse-width-modulated (PWM) drive signals with hardware-enforced dead-time insertion \(\InverterDeadTime\): \[ \InverterDeadTime \ge \MaximumTurnOffDelay + \GateDriverSkew - \MinimumTurnOnDelay + \DeadTimeSafetyMargin \] where \(\MaximumTurnOffDelay\) is the maximum turn-off delay of the power switch, \(\MinimumTurnOnDelay\) is the minimum turn-on delay, \(\GateDriverSkew\) is the propagation delay mismatch across isolated gate drivers, and \(\DeadTimeSafetyMargin\) is an engineering safety buffer.3

3 Inverter Dead-Time Insertion: Timer dead time is selected from switch and driver timing over temperature and load, then verified with the actual gate waveforms. An overlap can create a damaging bus short; a numeric current or fuse outcome requires the circuit impedance and protection response.

Modern motor control microcontrollers (such as STM32 Advanced-control timers TIM1/TIM8 or TI C2000 ePWM modules) synthesize PWM waveforms using hardware up-down counting counters (TIMx_CNT). In center-aligned mode, the counter counts up from 0 to the auto-reload value (TIMx_ARR) and back down to 0, producing symmetrical PWM waveforms with period \(\PwmPeriod = 2 \cdot \text{ARR} \cdot \TimerClockPeriod\). Center-aligned PWM provides symmetric switching and repeatable ADC trigger opportunities. Ripple reduction and valid sampling windows depend on duty cycle, modulation, current-sensing topology, and settling time. Synchronization alone does not remove switching noise.

The timer’s hardware Dead-Time Generator (DTG) monitors the compare match outputs (TIMx_CCRx) and inserts dead-time delay cycles \(\DeadTimeCycleCount\) using an autonomous digital delay line: \[ \InverterDeadTime = \DeadTimeCycleCount \cdot \DeadTimeTickPeriod \] By latching the DTG configuration behind hardware register lock bits (BDTR.LOCK), the microcontroller guarantees that the dead-time delay cannot be altered by runaway firmware or memory corruption. Once locked at boot, the register ignores subsequent software writes until the next hardware reset.

A power MOSFET or GaN FET switch possesses input capacitance \(\SwitchInputCapacitance = \GateSourceCapacitance + \MillerCapacitance\), where \(\MillerCapacitance\) is the non-linear reverse transfer (Miller) capacitance. During gate turn-on and turn-off, charge transfer follows: \[ \TotalGateCharge = \int_0^{\SwitchingTransitionTime} \GateCurrent(\TimeInstant) d\TimeInstant = \GateSourceCharge + \MillerCharge \] During turn-off, the gate driver sinks current through gate resistance \(\TurnOffGateResistance\). The gate-source voltage \(\GateSourceVoltage\) drops to the Miller plateau voltage \(\MillerPlateauVoltage\), where it dwells while the drain-to-source voltage \(\DrainSourceVoltage\) traverses the entire DC rail: \[ \MillerPlateauDuration = \frac{\MillerCharge}{\TurnOffGateCurrent} = \frac{\MillerCharge \cdot \TurnOffGateResistance}{\MillerPlateauVoltage - \NegativeGateSupplyVoltage} \] Turn-on and turn-off times depend on gate resistance, driver strength, load, and device charge. Neither is universally longer; dead time must use the measured worst-case mismatch.

If dead time is insufficient, both high-side and low-side switches conduct simultaneously (shoot-through), creating a low-impedance short circuit directly bridging the DC rail to ground: \[ \ShootThroughCurrent = \frac{\DcBusVoltage}{2 \SwitchOnResistance + \BridgeTraceResistance} \] This resistive expression gives a prospective current, not its instantaneous value. Loop inductance limits the initial rise, and the resulting device stress depends on switching overlap, protection delay, and the circuit safe operating area.

Even when dead time is configured correctly, half-bridge legs face parasitic Miller cross-conduction. When the high-side MOSFET turns on, the phase node voltage slews at high speed (\(d\DrainSourceVoltage/d\TimeInstant \sim 10\text{--}50\text{ V/ns}\) in modern SiC and GaN bridges). This steep voltage transient forces displacement current through the low-side switch’s Miller capacitance: \[ \MillerDisplacementCurrent = \MillerCapacitance \frac{d\DrainSourceVoltage}{d\TimeInstant} \] This current flows through the low-side gate resistor \(\TurnOffGateResistance\) back to ground, inducing a parasitic gate voltage rise: \[ \InducedGateVoltage = \MillerDisplacementCurrent \cdot \TurnOffGateResistance = \MillerCapacitance \frac{d\DrainSourceVoltage}{d\TimeInstant} \TurnOffGateResistance \] If \(\InducedGateVoltage\) exceeds the gate threshold voltage \(\TransistorThresholdVoltage\) (typically \(1.5\text{--}3.0\text{ V}\) in GaN FETs), the low-side switch turns on spuriously while the high-side switch is conducting, inducing catastrophic shoot-through. Power electronics gate drivers counter this using Active Miller Clamps (AMC), an internal low-impedance switch that clamps the gate directly to the negative rail whenever \(\GateSourceVoltage\) drops below \(1.0\text{ V}\), or split gate drives (\(\TurnOnGateResistance / \TurnOffGateResistance\)) with antiparallel Schottky diodes.

While necessary for silicon survival, dead time introduces non-linear voltage distortion into stator windings. During the dead-time interval \(\InverterDeadTime\), both active MOSFETs are commanded off, and signed instantaneous phase current \(\InstantaneousPhaseCurrent\) (positive from the inverter node into the winding) is forced to freewheel through antiparallel body diodes. The sign of the phase current determines whether the inverter phase node clamps to \(\DcBusVoltage\) or ground, producing an average phase voltage error: \[ \DeadTimeVoltageError = -\operatorname{sgn}(\InstantaneousPhaseCurrent) \cdot \frac{\InverterDeadTime}{\PwmPeriod} \DcBusVoltage \] In three-phase synchronous machines, this square-wave error injects 5th and 7th harmonic voltages into the stationary reference frame. When transformed into the rotor synchronous \(\Glyph{d}\Glyph{q}\) reference frame via Park’s transformation, these odd harmonics combine into a 6th harmonic voltage disturbance. The amplitudes and phases follow the harmonic coefficients of the phase-voltage error and the chosen transformation convention. They are not equal to the fundamental square-wave coefficient. The current loop filters these voltage harmonics before they become torque ripple, creating a corresponding 6th harmonic torque ripple: \[ \SixthHarmonicTorqueRipple(6\ElectricalRotorAngle) = \frac{3}{2} \RotorPolePairCount \, \PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage \cdot \FiniteChange \TorqueProducingCurrent(6\ElectricalRotorAngle) \] Left uncompensated, this hardware-induced torque ripple produces high-frequency torque chatter that excites mechanical drivetrain resonances. Modern motor drives implement dead-time compensation algorithms in low-level firmware that inject feedforward voltage vectors opposite to \(\DeadTimeDqVoltageError\), linearizing the actuator response for high-level ML policies.

Inductive Voltage Droop and Gate Delay Stretch

In data center machine learning clusters, power delivery networks (PDNs) are supported by large rack-level voltage regulator modules (VRMs) and extensive decoupling capacitor planes. On embedded physical AI compute platforms, deep learning accelerators (such as tensor cores and systolic array matrix engines) share tightly constrained power rails with CPU cores and real-time safety microcontrollers. When a deep neural network transitions from idle to full execution, current draw increases by tens of amperes within nanoseconds.

Power delivery network impedance and rail droop

A power delivery network (PDN) has parasitic resistance and inductance in each source path. For a lumped branch with resistance \(\PdnBranchResistance\), inductance \(\ParasiticLoopInductance\), and branch current \(\PdnBranchCurrent\), its voltage drop is: \[ \SourceVoltage-\AcceleratorRailVoltage = \ParasiticLoopInductance \frac{d\PdnBranchCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} + \PdnBranchCurrent \PdnBranchResistance \] The accelerator’s load current need not equal this branch current during a transient: local decoupling supplies their difference. The rail response follows the coupled source-path and capacitor equations of section 1.10. Substituting the full load-current slew into every upstream inductance would ignore that buffering.

To prevent rail excursions from exceeding allowable voltage tolerances \(\pm \AllowableRailExcursion = \NominalSupplyVoltage \times \RailVoltageTolerance\) (typically \(\pm 5\text{ percent}\), or \(45\text{ mV}\) on a \(0.9\text{ V}\) digital core rail), the power delivery network must maintain an impedance profile strictly below the target impedance \(\PdnTargetImpedance\) across the entire operational frequency band: \[ \PdnTargetImpedance = \frac{\NominalSupplyVoltage \cdot \RailVoltageTolerance}{\MaximumLoadCurrentStep} \] For a \(0.9\text{ V}\) accelerator drawing a \(50\text{ A}\) step transient with \(5\text{ percent}\) allowable ripple, \(\PdnTargetImpedance = 45\text{ mV} / 50\text{ A} = 0.90\text{ m}\OhmUnit\) from DC up to gigahertz frequencies. Exceeding this impedance at any excitation frequency permits transient load steps to pull the supply rail outside its timing-qualified window.

Switched-mode power supplies (VRMs) have limited feedback control loop bandwidth (\(100\text{ kHz}\) to \(1\text{ MHz}\)) and cannot supply charge during nanosecond current transitions. Energy must be buffered across a multi-tier decoupling capacitor hierarchy structured by frequency response:

  1. On-Die Decoupling (\(> 100\text{ MHz}\)): Deep trench and metal-insulator-metal (MIM) capacitors fabricated directly on the silicon die (\(\OnDieDecouplingCapacitance \sim 10\text{--}100\text{ nF}\), parasitic loop inductance \(< 10\text{ pH}\), response time \(< 0.5\text{ ns}\)) supply charge during the initial clock cycles.
  2. Package Decoupling (\(10\text{--}100\text{ MHz}\)): Land-side capacitors (LSC) mounted under the BGA package (\(\PackageDecouplingCapacitance \sim 1\text{--}10\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{F}\), loop inductance \(\sim 50\text{--}200\text{ pH}\), response time \(1\text{--}5\text{ ns}\)).
  3. Board-Level High-Frequency MLCCs (\(100\text{ kHz}\text{--}10\text{ MHz}\)): Low-ESL surface-mount multi-layer ceramic capacitors (0201/0402 case sizes, \(\DecouplingCapacitance \sim 0.1\text{--}10\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{F}\), loop inductance \(\sim 0.5\text{--}2\text{ nH}\), response time \(10\text{--}100\text{ ns}\)).
  4. Bulk Decoupling (\(\text{DC}\text{--}100\text{ kHz}\)): Polymer tantalum or aluminum electrolytic capacitors (\(\BulkDecouplingCapacitance \sim 100\text{--}1000\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{F}\), response time \(1\text{--}10\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)) sustain the rail until the active VRM regulation loop stabilizes.

Parasitic inductance and parallel capacitor branches can also create anti-resonance impedance peaks between their resonances. A simple LC frequency scale is \(\LcResonantFrequency=1/(2\pi\sqrt{\ParasiticLoopInductance \DecouplingCapacitance})\), but the peak magnitude requires the complete network, including capacitor equivalent series resistance and inductance. Excitation near a poorly damped peak can amplify supply excursions. A target-impedance check therefore needs the measured or modeled impedance spectrum, not capacitance alone.

Digital logic gate delay and the alpha-power law

When a \(0.90\text{ V}\) core rail sags by \(60\text{ mV}\) during an accelerator burst, transistor drive current drops and every logic gate along a critical path switches more slowly, eating into register setup slack. In sub-micron CMOS silicon, logic gate propagation delay \(\GatePropagationDelay\) is governed by the Sakurai-Newton alpha-power law:4 \[ \GatePropagationDelay(\SupplyVoltage) \propto \frac{\SwitchedCapacitance \cdot \SupplyVoltage}{(\SupplyVoltage - \TransistorThresholdVoltage)^\VelocitySaturationExponent} \] where \(\SwitchedCapacitance\) is the gate load capacitance, \(\TransistorThresholdVoltage\) is the transistor threshold voltage, and \(\VelocitySaturationExponent \in [1.2, 1.5]\) is the velocity saturation index (contrasting with \(\VelocitySaturationExponent = 2.0\) in long-channel theory).

4 Sakurai-Newton Delay Modeling: The alpha-power law models voltage-dependent gate delay; its parameters are technology dependent. The model predicts reduced setup slack during droop, while a missed deadline, register error, or reset must be established against measured path slack and device thresholds.

When rail voltage droops from \(\NominalSupplyVoltage\) to \(\DroopedSupplyVoltage\), propagation delay stretches according to: \[ \frac{\GatePropagationDelay(\DroopedSupplyVoltage)}{\GatePropagationDelay(\NominalSupplyVoltage)} = \frac{\DroopedSupplyVoltage}{\NominalSupplyVoltage} \left( \frac{\NominalSupplyVoltage - \TransistorThresholdVoltage}{\DroopedSupplyVoltage - \TransistorThresholdVoltage} \right)^\VelocitySaturationExponent \] In a synchronous digital pipeline with clock period \(\ClockPeriod\), setup time \(\RegisterSetupTime\), and clock skew \(\PipelineClockSkew\), timing closure requires: \[ \GatePropagationDelay + \RegisterSetupTime + \PipelineClockSkew \le \ClockPeriod \] The following illustrative calculation evaluates the delay ratio and remaining path slack with the same voltage-dependent model. A droop consumes setup margin; whether it causes a timing violation depends on the original slack, clock response, and characterized process corner.

For a drop from 0.90 V to 0.81 V, with threshold 0.35 V and exponent 1.3, delay rises by 13.5 percent to 908.3 ps. Starting with 150 ps of slack leaves 41.7 ps. These assumptions therefore do not predict a setup violation. Some processors respond to droop with active clock stretching, increasing the clock period while the rail recovers. The supported response, detection delay, and resulting execution-time stretch require device-specific characterization. Thermal and Timing Coupling connects rail disturbance to the permission task’s timing budget.

DRAM Contention and Interconnect Arbitration

In cloud machine learning clusters, GPUs use high-bandwidth memory (HBM) physically separated from host CPU memory. This separates some accelerator traffic from host DRAM, although accelerator clients still contend for those channels. In embedded robotics SoCs, however, system architectures rely on Unified Memory Architecture (UMA) to satisfy size, weight, and power (SWaP) constraints. Application CPU cores, neural accelerators, GPU cores, and high-rate PCIe or CSI camera DMA engines all share a single physical DRAM interface. In these shared-memory multi-core platforms, DMA transfers and vision prefetching compete directly with the permission path for memory controller command queues and physical DRAM banks.

DRAM bank organization and conflict latency

DRAM organizes storage into channels, ranks, bank groups, and banks; their counts and timing depend on the memory standard and device organization. Each individual bank possesses an independent row buffer consisting of sense amplifiers. A read that changes the open row can require the following sequence:

  1. Row precharge (\(\RowPrechargeTime\)): Closes the active row and equalizes the bitlines for the next activation (18.75 ns per bank at LPDDR5-6400).
  2. Row activate (\(\RowActivationDelay\)): Reads an entire page of storage cells (e.g., 2 KB to 8 KB) across wordlines into the bank’s open row buffer (18.75 ns at LPDDR5-6400).
  3. Column access (read latency, \(\ColumnAccessLatency\)): Reads or writes data beats from the open row buffer onto the memory data bus. LPDDR5 specifies this latency in clock cycles that depend on the data rate, so a row hit pays only this step.

To sustain high data rates, modern DRAM organizes banks into bank groups. An LPDDR5 channel offers three bank organizations: 16 banks divided into 4 bank groups for the higher data rates, 16 independent banks, or 8 larger banks formed by merging pairs (Steiner et al. 2022). In bank-group mode, consecutive column commands to the same bank group must wait a longer column-to-column delay \(\IntraGroupColumnDelay\) than commands to different groups, which need only \(\InterGroupColumnDelay\), because banks in one group share internal data paths. An address mapping that interleaves consecutive bursts across bank groups hides the longer spacing; one that keeps a stream inside a single group exposes it.

Steiner, Lukas, Matthias Jung, Michael Huonker, and Norbert Wehn. 2022. “Unveiling the Real Performance of LPDDR5 Memories.” Proceedings of the International Symposium on Memory Systems (MEMSYS), 1–3. https://doi.org/10.1145/3565053.3565062.

When consecutive memory operations target different rows within the same physical bank (a bank conflict), the memory controller cannot immediately access the column. It must execute a precharge followed by an activate before issuing the column command, introducing a compulsory stall: \[ \BankConflictPenalty \approx \RowPrechargeTime + \RowActivationDelay \] At LPDDR5-6400 that stall is 37.5 ns. The minimum time between consecutive row activations to the same bank is also governed by the row cycle time \(\RowCycleTime = \RowActiveTime + \RowPrechargeTime\), where \(\RowActiveTime\) is the minimum time a row must stay open. Repeated conflicts in one bank are therefore spaced at least \(\RowCycleTime\) apart.

Memory controllers select between an open-page or closed-page policy to balance row-buffer locality against thrashing. Under an open-page policy, row buffers remain active after access in anticipation of spatial locality. While this maximizes bandwidth for sequential streaming (such as dense general matrix multiplication (GEMM) weight fetching or linear camera DMA writes), sporadic random accesses (such as sensor fusion state lookups or 1 kHz safety monitor checks) frequently hit different rows in active banks. When a continuous camera DMA stream writes video frames to the same DRAM bank that holds real-time safety buffers, repeated row-buffer conflicts transform low-latency reads into multi-hundred-nanosecond stalls.5

5 DRAM Bank-Conflict Penalties: A row change in one bank can add precharge and activate time. Video DMA can also queue transactions ahead of a safety read. The resulting delay must be bounded for the specific address mapping, arbitration policy, and workload; a fixed penalty per request is generally unjustified.

Modern DRAM controllers employ First-Ready First-Come-First-Served (FR-FCFS) command scheduling. FR-FCFS prioritizes column read/write requests that hit currently open row buffers over older requests that require row precharge and activation, maximizing overall DRAM bus throughput. However, under high cognitive load, a continuous flood of vision DMA requests or matrix multiplication memory bursts will repeatedly hit open rows. FR-FCFS schedulers then systematically postpone isolated, latency-critical read requests issued by real-time safety threads, potentially starving those requests unless the controller enforces age or service bounds.

Interconnect crossbars and burst contention

On-chip interconnect fabrics (such as ARM CoreLink AXI4 crossbars or Network-on-Chip (NoC) meshes) split high-bandwidth accelerator and camera DMA transactions into bounded bursts of up to \(\AxiBurstLength = 256\) beats. In a shared crossbar without real-time prioritization, transactions enter a FIFO or weighted round-robin queue. For \(\QueuedTransactionCount\) competing accelerator transactions admitted ahead of a safety-critical permission request, the queuing transfer latency is: \[ \IdealMemoryQueueDelay = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^\QueuedTransactionCount {\DataVolume}_{k}}{\SharedBusBandwidth} \] Adding physical bank-switch penalties \(\sum \BankConflictPenalty\), device-specific refresh overhead, and bus turnaround penalties increases the safety read latency. Refresh mode, temperature, address mapping, and controller policy determine the bound. To protect real-time control paths against memory contention, embedded systems implement three structural defenses:

  1. AXI Quality of Service (QoS) Priority Elevation: The AxQOS field communicates a requested priority. The interconnect and memory controller must implement a documented service policy; the field alone does not guarantee preemption or bounded latency. Already issued transactions and refresh can still block a safety read.
  2. DRAM Bank Partitioning and Page Coloring: The operating system memory allocator allocates physical memory pages such that real-time safety code and high-rate camera DMA buffers map to strictly disjoint physical DRAM banks, removing those bank conflicts when the physical address mapping is known and enforced. Shared channels, refresh, and controller queues remain.
  3. On-Chip SRAM Isolation: Critical safety states, barrier function parameters, and active trajectory splines bypass external DRAM entirely, executing exclusively out of static on-chip SRAM or Data Tightly Coupled Memory (DTCM).

Clock Synchronization and Network Timestamps

In distributed machine learning clusters, nodes synchronize system clocks via Network Time Protocol (NTP) primarily for coarse event logging and telemetry, while parallel training algorithms synchronize execution through communication barriers (such as NCCL AllReduce). In physical AI systems, however, time directly maps to physical space: when a robot moves at \(30\text{ m/s}\), a \(1\text{ ms}\) synchronization error produces \(3\text{ cm}\) of spatial displacement, warping sensor fusion point clouds and invalidating safety clearance buffers. Distributed perception pipelines must convert sensor timestamps into a common clock domain with a measured conversion-error bound.

Precision time protocol message exchange

The following end-to-end delay exchange illustrates IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol (PTP), synchronizing a follower clock (\(\Glyph{S}\)) to a leader clock (\(\Glyph{M}\)). IEEE 802.1AS uses a related timing architecture with peer-delay measurement; its exchange is not identical:

  1. \(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_1\): Master transmits a Sync frame, recording its local transmission timestamp.
  2. \(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_2\): Slave receives the Sync frame, recording its local arrival timestamp.
  3. \(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_3\): Slave transmits a Delay_Req frame, recording its local transmission timestamp.
  4. \(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_4\): Master receives the Delay_Req frame, recording its local arrival timestamp.

Under the assumption of clock stability and symmetric forward and reverse propagation delay (\(\PtpForwardPathDelay = \PtpReversePathDelay = \PtpPropagationDelay\)), the time relationships satisfy: \[ \PtpExchangeTimestamp_2 = \PtpExchangeTimestamp_1 + \ClockOffset + \PtpPropagationDelay \] \[ \PtpExchangeTimestamp_4 = \PtpExchangeTimestamp_3 - \ClockOffset + \PtpPropagationDelay \] where \(\ClockOffset\) is the slave-minus-master clock offset. The estimated clock offset is: \[ \ClockOffset = \frac{(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_2 - \PtpExchangeTimestamp_1) - (\PtpExchangeTimestamp_4 - \PtpExchangeTimestamp_3)}{2} \] The one-way network propagation delay is: \[ \PtpPropagationDelay = \frac{(\PtpExchangeTimestamp_2 - \PtpExchangeTimestamp_1) + (\PtpExchangeTimestamp_4 - \PtpExchangeTimestamp_3)}{2} \]

Slave clocks adjust their local time base using a closed-loop digital proportional-integral (PI) clock servo filter. At synchronization step \(\SynchronizationStepIndex\), the measured offset \(\ClockOffset[\SynchronizationStepIndex]\) drives frequency correction \(\ServoFrequencyCorrection[\SynchronizationStepIndex]\) applied to the hardware accumulator register: \[ \ServoFrequencyCorrection[\SynchronizationStepIndex] = -\PidProportionalGain \ClockOffset[\SynchronizationStepIndex] - \PidIntegralGain \sum_{j=0}^\SynchronizationStepIndex \ClockOffset[\PastSynchronizationStepIndex] \] Here \(\ServoFrequencyCorrection\) is added to the follower frequency, \(\PidProportionalGain,\PidIntegralGain>0\), and the sampling interval is absorbed into \(\PidIntegralGain\). A positive follower-minus-leader offset therefore slows the follower; using the opposite hardware correction convention reverses the sign.

If forward and reverse path delays are asymmetric (\(\PathDelayAsymmetry = \PtpForwardPathDelay - \PtpReversePathDelay \ne 0\)), the calculated offset incurs an unobservable systematic bias of \(\PathDelayAsymmetry / 2\). Residual clock uncertainty \(\ClockConversionBound\), combining asymmetric propagation, oscillator frequency drift between sync intervals, and hardware timestamp quantization, is explicitly bounded: \[ \ClockConversionBound \le \frac{|\PathDelayAsymmetry|}{2} + \SyncMessageInterval \cdot \left| \frac{\ResidualFrequencyError}{\NominalOscillatorFrequency} \right| + 2 \TimestampQuantizationBound \] Here \(\ResidualFrequencyError\) bounds residual frequency error after servo correction, rather than the applied correction itself; \(\TimestampQuantizationBound\) bounds timestamp quantization. Additional timestamp-reference and servo errors must be included if they are not covered by these terms. This conversion bound is stored directly in the proposal header’s sync_error_ns field (table 10) and added to every evidence-age calculation in the 1 kHz permission path: \[ \ObservationAge = (\CurrentWallClockTime - \EvidenceEpoch) + \ClockConversionBound \]

Physical layer hardware timestamping

Software-based timestamping captures timestamps inside the OS network socket layer or interrupt service routine. It suffers from variable kernel queueing, driver interrupt latency, and thread scheduling jitter, which inject \(50\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) to \(5\text{ ms}\) of non-deterministic timing error. To eliminate host dispatch error, physical AI hardware uses Physical Layer (PHY) Hardware Timestamping.6 Dedicated timestamping units in the Ethernet PHY or media access control (MAC) controller capture hardware counter values at the exact instant the physical Start of Frame Delimiter (SFD) crosses the Media Independent Interface (MII / RGMII). This removes software dispatch from the capture point. Timestamp resolution, reference-plane calibration, and residual error remain device-specific and must be measured.

6 Hardware Timestamping: A compatible PHY or MAC timestamps frames closer to the wire than a software interrupt handler. Hardware timestamping removes one source of dispatch variation, while path asymmetry and clock conversion remain. Timestamp uncertainty belongs in the physical belief record and permission budget.

Heterogeneous SoC Mailboxes, Memory Barriers, and Zero-Copy IPC

Large sensor buffers can consume substantial shared-memory bandwidth when each pipeline stage makes another copy. A copy may generate both a read and a write of the payload, depending on cache residency and the transfer path. On a heterogeneous robotics SoC, that traffic can contend with a safety processor’s memory accesses. Shared buffers reduce copying, but require explicit ownership and publication ordering. Small proposal records may instead use a bounded copy into protected memory. The mechanisms below distinguish four contexts. A discrete safety microcontroller receives proposals over a board-level link. Application and safety cores on one die exchange platform-specific mailboxes. Linux processes share coherent memory. Supported GPU processes share device allocations. Their ordering and lifetime contracts are not interchangeable.

Board-level links into a local mailbox

The machine in this book keeps its safety microcontroller on a separate chip with its own memory (Silicon Isolation Decisions), so no memory is shared with the application processor and nothing in this subsection depends on the host’s cache or interconnect.

  1. The application processor serializes the proposal into a frame that carries a sequence number, the evidence and issue timestamps, and a CRC, and sends it over a board-level link such as SPI, a UART, or CAN FD.
  2. The microcontroller’s link peripheral writes the incoming bytes into a receive buffer in its own SRAM by DMA. A double buffer lets the DMA fill one half while firmware reads the other.
  3. The DMA-complete interrupt checks the frame’s length, CRC, and sequence number, then publishes the proposal into the seqlock mailbox of algorithm 1. The writer and the reader, the permission tick, both run on the microcontroller.
  4. The permission tick makes one bounded read of that mailbox and checks age, expiry, and permission before using the payload.

The link is a black channel in the sense of the functional-safety fieldbus profiles (International Electrotechnical Commission 2021). Corruption, repetition, loss, and delay are detected end to end by the frame’s CRC, sequence number, and timestamps and by the lease, so the link’s hardware and drivers need not be trusted for correctness. Its timing is another matter.
