Serialization at the link rate, DMA completion, and receive-interrupt latency age every newly admitted proposal and must be bounded and charged to the dispatch-to-admission interval.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2021. IEC 61784-3:2021: Industrial Communication Networks, Profiles, Part 3: Functional Safety Fieldbuses, General Rules and Profile Definitions. International Electrotechnical Commission.

Remote processor messaging over shared SRAM

When one system-on-chip hosts both domains, the shared-die placement of Two Paths and the Resources They Share, communication between Linux application cores and a real-time safety core relies on shared SRAM mailboxes signaled via hardware doorbells:

  1. Core A (Application Processor) writes a message payload to a fixed buffer in shared SRAM.
  2. Core A makes the payload visible to Core B using the platform’s coherent mapping or explicit cache maintenance and ordering.
  3. Core A triggers an inter-processor interrupt (IPI) by writing to Core B’s hardware mailbox register.
  4. Core B checks publication order, integrity, age, and permission before using the payload.

Linux RPMsg is a virtio-based messaging framework that may use shared memory and a platform-specific kick. It provides standard transport abstraction but does not prescribe this fixed-buffer protocol or a universal latency bound. It applies only to this shared-die case; a discrete microcontroller shares no memory to message through.

CPU pipeline reordering and memory fences

In weakly ordered memory architectures (such as ARMv8-A and ARMv9-A), processors do not guarantee that memory writes will be observed by other cores in program order. Out-of-order execution engines and asynchronous write buffers can reorder stores to non-dependent addresses:

// Core A (Application Processor); q_target and q_cmd are joint_target_t structs
shared_buffer->q_target = q_cmd; // Store 1: Payload (struct copy)
mailbox_doorbell = 1;            // Store 2: Interrupt trigger

If the CPU reorders Store 2 ahead of Store 1, Core B receives the interrupt before the new joint target is committed to physical memory. The consumer then reads stale or partially written struct fields across the mailbox boundary.

To enforce deterministic ordering across heterogeneous cores, developers must insert architectural hardware memory barriers:

shared_buffer->q_target = q_cmd; // Struct copy of the joint targets
__asm__ volatile("dmb oshst" ::: "memory"); // Outer-shareable store barrier
mailbox_doorbell = 1;            // Assert doorbell register

In ARM architectures, memory barriers and instructions are distinguished by scope and execution semantics:

  • DMB (Data Memory Barrier): Ensures that memory accesses specified before the barrier appear in memory before memory accesses specified after the barrier. It does not block execution of non-memory instructions.
  • DSB (Data Synchronization Barrier): Waits for completion of the memory accesses and maintenance operations covered by its type and scope before subsequent instructions execute.
  • ISB (Instruction Synchronization Barrier): Flushes the CPU instruction prefetch pipeline, ensuring subsequent instructions are fetched anew from cache or memory.

Barriers operate across explicit architectural shareability domains:

  • DMB ISHST (Inner Shareable Store): Orders stores across cores within the local application CPU cluster sharing a coherent L2/L3 cache.
  • DMB OSHST / DMB OSH (Outer Shareable): Orders accesses in the configured outer-shareable domain; membership depends on the SoC, and ordering does not create coherence.
  • DMB SY (System): Enforces ordering across all system masters and peripherals on the SoC.

Cache maintenance is necessary when the communicating agents do not share a coherent mapping. The producer cleans dirty payload lines, completes the required maintenance, and orders publication. The consumer invalidates stale lines before reading under the platform contract. A supported non-cacheable mapping can avoid those cache operations, but does not make barriers single-cycle or remove interconnect contention. Device memory has different access restrictions from Normal memory and is not an interchangeable choice for arbitrary structure copies. Alignment must match the actual cache-line size. Lock-free and wait-free mailbox exchanges and Two Paths and the Resources They Share connect these mechanisms to the proposal boundary.7

7 Hardware Memory Barriers: Barriers order accesses only within their specified shareability and memory-attribute contract. On a noncoherent route, cache clean/invalidate operations or coherent mappings are also required. Arm’s memory-ordering guide distinguishes ordering from coherence.

POSIX shared memory and lock-free SPSC ring buffers

Within user-space Linux runtimes, independent operating system processes (such as a vision preprocessing daemon, a multimodal VLA policy inference server, and a high-rate state estimator) exchange telemetry and sensor streams. They avoid kernel socket copies by using POSIX Shared Memory (shm_open and mmap):

  1. A producer process creates a POSIX shared memory object using shm_open("/robot_telemetry", O_CREAT | O_RDWR, 0660) located in the RAM-backed tmpfs filesystem (/dev/shm), bypassing physical storage media entirely.
  2. Sizing the segment via ftruncate(fd, sizeof(telemetry_ring_buffer_t)) establishes its length; physical backing is generally allocated when pages are faulted in.
  3. Both producer and consumer processes map the memory into their respective virtual address spaces via mmap(NULL, sizeof(...), PROT_READ | PROT_WRITE, MAP_SHARED, fd, 0).
  4. Each process prefaults and successfully locks its mapping with mlock() before cyclic execution. This prevents swapping of those pages; mapping changes and copy-on-write behavior still need explicit control.
  5. Position-Independent Memory Layout: Because Address Space Layout Randomization (ASLR) and independent virtual memory spaces cause mmap() to return different base virtual addresses in each process, data structures placed in shared memory must never store absolute virtual pointers (void* or raw references). All references within shared buffers must be position-independent, structured as relative byte offsets, flat fixed-size arrays, or integer indices.

When streaming sequential sensor frames or trajectory waypoints across processes, mutual-exclusion mutexes introduce non-deterministic blocking and priority inversion. A lock-free single-producer single-consumer (SPSC) ring buffer coordinates one producer and one consumer using acquire-release atomic indices. The following C++ sketch assumes lock-free atomics, bounded nonthrowing copies of T, and coherent memory. Cross-process use additionally requires a platform-supported shared atomic representation and one-time construction before either process accesses it:

#include <atomic>
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstddef>
#include <new>

template <typename T, size_t Capacity>
class LockFreeSpscRingBuffer {
    static_assert(Capacity > 0 && (Capacity & (Capacity - 1)) == 0,
                  "Capacity must be a nonzero power of two");
    static_assert(std::atomic<uint64_t>::is_always_lock_free,
                  "Index atomics must be lock-free on this target");

private:
    // Align head and tail to distinct 64-byte cache lines to eliminate false sharing
    alignas(64) std::atomic<uint64_t> write_index_{0};
    alignas(64) std::atomic<uint64_t> read_index_{0};
    alignas(64) T storage_[Capacity];

public:
    bool Push(const T& item) {
        const uint64_t current_write = write_index_.load(std::memory_order_relaxed);
        const uint64_t current_read = read_index_.load(std::memory_order_acquire);

        if (current_write - current_read >= Capacity) {
            return false; // Buffer full: non-blocking drop or backpressure
        }

        // Fast bitwise masking replacing expensive modulo division
        storage_[current_write & (Capacity - 1)] = item;

        // Release order ensures item stores are committed before advancing pointer
        write_index_.store(current_write + 1, std::memory_order_release);
        return true;
    }

    bool Pop(T& item) {
        const uint64_t current_read = read_index_.load(std::memory_order_relaxed);
        const uint64_t current_write = write_index_.load(std::memory_order_acquire);

        if (current_read == current_write) {
            return false; // Buffer empty
        }

        item = storage_[current_read & (Capacity - 1)];

        // Release order publishes free slot back to producer
        read_index_.store(current_read + 1, std::memory_order_release);
        return true;
    }
};

Separating the indices onto different cache lines avoids false sharing between the two independently written counters on a target with the assumed line size. Each side still reads the other’s counter, and payload ownership still moves between cores, so coherence traffic remains. Power-of-two capacity permits masking for wraparound. Neither alignment nor this indexing choice establishes a cycle-time bound without target measurements.

Multi-rate synchronization: Double-buffering versus triple-buffering

In cyber-physical machines, producer and consumer tasks rarely execute at the same frequency. A multimodal vision-language-action policy may produce candidate trajectory chunks at \(20\text{ Hz}\) (\(50\text{ ms}\) period) with variable inference jitter (\(\pm 15\text{ ms}\)). The downstream safety permission filter and motor tracking loop, by contrast, execute strictly at \(1\text{ kHz}\) (\(1\text{ ms}\) period). Double-buffering separates a buffer being written from one being read, but it also needs an ownership protocol. If the producer finishes while the consumer still owns the old front buffer, the producer must wait, drop an update, or acquire another buffer. Rate mismatch alone does not make double-buffering incorrect. Allowing a writer to reuse a reader-owned buffer does.

Triple-buffering provides a private producer buffer, a private consumer buffer, and an intermediate buffer holding the latest completed update. Publication atomically exchanges the producer buffer with the intermediate buffer. At a tick boundary, the consumer may exchange its private buffer with the intermediate buffer, then read its new private buffer without concurrent writes. The exchanged state must include the new-data flag so that publication and ownership remain one atomic operation.

This is a latest-value channel: intermediate updates may be overwritten before the consumer acquires them. A queue is needed when every update must be retained. A compare-and-swap retry loop is lock-free only under its progress assumptions and is not automatically wait-free. The implementation must establish ordering, buffer lifetime, and a bounded reader attempt on the target. A failed attempt leaves the current record subject to its existing evidence and expiry checks.

CUDA inter-process communication for multi-accelerator pipelines

On high-throughput robotics platforms (such as autonomous mobile robots equipped with multiple GMSL2 camera clusters and lidar arrays), raw perception data streams into accelerator GPU memory for tensor processing. In modular physical AI software architectures, perception preprocessing (e.g., optical flow, spatial unprojection, and feature extraction) and foundation policy inference often execute in separate Linux processes for fault containment. Passing multi-gigabyte tensor buffers between processes via host system RAM consumes tens of gigabytes per second of shared DRAM bandwidth, precipitating memory bus contention.

To eliminate host DRAM staging entirely, CUDA Inter-Process Communication (CUDA IPC) enables direct peer-to-peer GPU memory access across distinct Linux process address spaces:

// Process A (Perception Producer): Export GPU device memory handle
float *d_camera_tensor;
cudaMalloc((void **)&d_camera_tensor, TENSOR_BYTES);

// Allocate and capture an opaque 64-byte IPC memory handle
cudaIpcMemHandle_t ipc_mem_handle;
cudaIpcGetMemHandle(&ipc_mem_handle, (void *)d_camera_tensor);

// Send the 64-byte handle to Process B via POSIX shm or UNIX domain socket
send_ipc_handle_to_consumer(&ipc_mem_handle);

In Process B (Policy Consumer), the exported handle is mapped directly into its local GPU virtual address space without copying:

// Process B (Policy Consumer): Map exported GPU memory directly
cudaIpcMemHandle_t received_handle;
receive_ipc_handle_from_producer(&received_handle);

float *d_mapped_tensor;
cudaIpcOpenMemHandle((void **)&d_mapped_tensor, received_handle,
                     cudaIpcMemLazyEnablePeerAccess);

// Wait on this publication's imported completion event before reading.
// Launch inference kernel directly referencing Process A's physical GPU memory
launch_vla_encoder_kernel<<<grid, block, 0, compute_stream>>>(d_mapped_tensor);

// At shutdown, finish outstanding work before releasing the mapping.
cudaStreamSynchronize(compute_stream);
cudaIpcCloseMemHandle((void *)d_mapped_tensor);

CUDA IPC requires compatible devices, drivers, and operating-system support. An imported allocation must remain alive until every importer finishes and closes its mapping. Publication requires explicit synchronization, not just handle transfer. CUDA IPC relies on strict memory allocation invariants: exported memory must be allocated as device memory via cudaMalloc() within the GPU’s physical device memory. Managed memory (cudaMallocManaged) and host pinned memory are explicitly unsupported by the CUDA IPC subsystem. On supported same-host multi-accelerator topologies, an imported allocation may be accessed through peer-to-peer links. CUDA IPC is not a transport between arbitrary independent SoCs. Rather than copying tensor buffers through host DRAM, the GPU hardware memory management unit (MMU) routes memory transactions directly across high-speed interconnect links. This eliminates host CPU DRAM double-buffering and saves tens of gigabytes per second of shared bus bandwidth.

To synchronize GPU execution across processes without round-tripping to host CPU threads, CUDA IPC exports cross-process CUDA event synchronization primitives via cudaIpcGetEventHandle() and cudaIpcOpenEventHandle(). Process A creates an interprocess event with timing disabled, records it after producing the buffer, and publishes the corresponding completion generation. The consumer must wait on that generation before using the data, and the producer must not reuse the buffer before the consumer finishes. Process B imports the event handle and enqueues a hardware barrier on its local stream: \[ \text{cudaStreamWaitEvent}(\text{consumer\_stream}, \text{imported\_event}, 0) \] Once the host has enqueued the dependency, later consumer-stream work waits for the recorded producer event without a host polling loop. Setup and submission still involve the CPU, and latency remains platform dependent. The CUDA IPC documentation specifies supported allocation and event types.

Actuator Electrical and Thermal Dynamics

In reinforcement learning and simulation environments (such as MuJoCo or Isaac Gym), policy actions are typically abstracted as instantaneous torque or position commands applied at discrete simulation steps. In physical systems, however, actuators convert electrical energy into mechanical work through electromagnetic Lorentz forces, subject to coupled electrical, thermal, and kinematic constraints.

Stator electrical rise time

A brushless permanent-magnet synchronous motor (PMSM / BLDC) phase winding has phase resistance \(\PhaseResistance\) and phase inductance \(\PhaseInductance\). Applying terminal voltage \(\WindingTerminalVoltage\) against back-electromotive force \(\BackEmfVoltage = \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed\) yields the first-order stator current ODE: \[ \PhaseInductance \frac{d\InstantaneousPhaseCurrent(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} + \PhaseResistance\, \InstantaneousPhaseCurrent(\TimeInstant) = \WindingTerminalVoltage - \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed \]

In three-phase synchronous machines under Field-Oriented Control (FOC), the stator currents are transformed from the three-phase \(\Glyph{a} \Glyph{b} \Glyph{c}\) frame into the orthogonal rotor synchronous \(\Glyph{d}\Glyph{q}\) reference frame via the Clarke and Park transformations: \[ \DirectAxisVoltage = \PhaseResistance \DirectAxisCurrent + \DirectAxisInductance \frac{d\DirectAxisCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} - \ElectricalAngularSpeed \QuadratureAxisInductance \TorqueProducingCurrent \] \[ \QuadratureAxisVoltage = \PhaseResistance \TorqueProducingCurrent + \QuadratureAxisInductance \frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} + \ElectricalAngularSpeed \DirectAxisInductance \DirectAxisCurrent + \ElectricalAngularSpeed \PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage \] The \(\Glyph{d}\) axis rotates with the rotor magnet flux, and the \(\Glyph{q}\) axis lies perpendicular to it. This separates flux and torque-producing current. Here \(\ElectricalAngularSpeed = \RotorPolePairCount \RotorAngularSpeed\) is electrical angular velocity (\(\RotorPolePairCount\) pole pairs), \(\PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage\) is the rotor permanent-magnet flux linkage, and \(\DirectAxisInductance, \QuadratureAxisInductance\) are direct and quadrature axis inductances. Under standard constant torque-angle control (\(\DirectAxisCurrent = 0\)), electromagnetic torque is directly proportional to quadrature current: \[ \MotorTorque = \frac{3}{2} \RotorPolePairCount \, \PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage \TorqueProducingCurrent = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent \]

For a commanded voltage step with the rotor held at rest (\(\RotorAngularSpeed = 0\)) and zero initial current, quadrature current rises exponentially: \[ \TorqueProducingCurrent(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\WindingTerminalVoltage}{\PhaseResistance} \left( 1 - e^{-\TimeInstant / \ElectricalTimeConstant} \right), \quad \ElectricalTimeConstant = \frac{\QuadratureAxisInductance}{\PhaseResistance} \] where \(\ElectricalTimeConstant\) is the electrical time constant. Current, and therefore delivered torque, reaches approximately 63 percent of its steady-state value after one time constant \(\ElectricalTimeConstant\) (Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping computes it for the running machine’s hub motors from their datasheet inductance and resistance). An unprivileged neural policy step command cannot produce an instantaneous step in physical torque.

Furthermore, achievable current is constrained by inverter voltage saturation: \[ \sqrt{\DirectAxisVoltage^2 + \QuadratureAxisVoltage^2} \le \frac{\DcBusVoltage}{\sqrt{3}} \] As rotor speed \(\RotorAngularSpeed\) rises, back-EMF \(\ElectricalAngularSpeed \PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage\) increases, consuming available quadrature voltage \(\QuadratureAxisVoltage\). At the actuator’s base speed, voltage headroom vanishes, and torque collapses unless negative direct-axis current (\(\DirectAxisCurrent < 0\), flux weakening) is commanded to oppose part of the magnet flux linkage, within current and magnet-demagnetization limits.

Lumped thermal networks and stator Joule heating

Current flowing through stator windings dissipates resistive Joule heat: \[ \WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) = \frac{3}{2} \TorqueProducingCurrent^2(\TimeInstant) \PhaseResistance(\WindingTemperature) \] where copper electrical resistance increases linearly with temperature: \[ \PhaseResistance(\WindingTemperature) = \ReferenceWindingResistance \left[ 1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient (\WindingTemperature - \CopperReferenceTemperature) \right] \] with temperature coefficient \(\CopperTemperatureCoefficient \approx 0.00393\text{ K}^{-1}\) for copper. At an operating temperature of \(150^\circ\text{C}\), stator copper resistance is approximately \(50\text{ percent}\) higher than at room temperature (\(25^\circ\text{C}\)). To sustain the same commanded torque \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\), the motor requires identical current \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\) but generates \(50\text{ percent}\) more thermal dissipation. This creates positive feedback between winding temperature and Joule heating that accelerates thermal degradation.

In a physical motor, multi-node lumped thermal networks span multiple physical interfaces with disparate thermal masses:

  1. Stator Winding Copper: Low thermal capacitance \(\CopperThermalCapacitance\), rapid heating from Joule dissipation (\(\CopperThermalTimeConstant \sim 10\text{--}30\text{ s}\)).
  2. Stator Iron Laminations: Moderate thermal capacitance \(\IronThermalCapacitance\), absorbing iron eddy-current and hysteresis losses \(\IronLossPower\) and heat conducted across winding insulation \(\CopperIronThermalResistance\). The iron losses depend on flux, frequency, and material.
  3. Motor Frame and Aluminum Housing: Large thermal capacitance \(\CaseThermalCapacitance\), conducting heat into the robotic link structure and convecting to ambient air through \(\CaseAmbientThermalResistance\) (\(\CaseThermalTimeConstant \sim 10\text{--}30\text{ min}\)).

The temperature dynamics across these nodes satisfy the coupled differential system: \[ \CopperThermalCapacitance \frac{d\WindingTemperature}{d\TimeInstant} = \WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) - \frac{\WindingTemperature - \StatorIronTemperature}{\CopperIronThermalResistance} \] \[ \IronThermalCapacitance \frac{d\StatorIronTemperature}{d\TimeInstant} = \frac{\WindingTemperature - \StatorIronTemperature}{\CopperIronThermalResistance} + \IronLossPower - \frac{\StatorIronTemperature - \CaseTemperature}{\IronCaseThermalResistance} \] \[ \CaseThermalCapacitance \frac{d\CaseTemperature}{d\TimeInstant} = \frac{\StatorIronTemperature - \CaseTemperature}{\IronCaseThermalResistance} - \frac{\CaseTemperature - \AmbientTemperature}{\CaseAmbientThermalResistance} \]

For initial temperature at ambient, a simplified single-node lumped thermal model uses effective thermal resistance to ambient \(\ThermalResistance\) (in \(\text{K/W}\)) and thermal heat capacity \(\StatorThermalCapacitance\) (in \(\text{J/K}\)). The temperature rise \(\JouleanTemperatureRise(\TimeInstant) = \WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant) - \AmbientTemperature\) satisfies:8 \[ \StatorThermalCapacitance \frac{\DerivativeOperator\JouleanTemperatureRise(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} = \WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) - \frac{\JouleanTemperatureRise(\TimeInstant)}{\ThermalResistance} \] Under constant power, temperature rises with thermal time constant \(\ThermalTimeConstant = \ThermalResistance \StatorThermalCapacitance\): \[ \JouleanTemperatureRise(\TimeInstant) = \WindingLossPower \ThermalResistance \left( 1 - e^{-\TimeInstant / \ThermalTimeConstant} \right) \]

8 Stator Thermal Time Constants: Resistive Joule heating elevates motor phase winding temperatures during continuous high-torque maneuvers. Because copper electrical resistivity increases linearly with temperature, hot windings have higher resistance, reducing torque output under terminal voltage saturation. Exceeding winding insulation temperature limits breaks down dielectric coatings, causing inter-turn short circuits and permanent winding damage.

Continuous torque capacity \(\ContinuousTorque\) is bounded by the maximum allowable winding temperature rise \(\AllowableWindingTemperatureRise\): \[ \ContinuousTorque \le \MotorTorqueConstant \sqrt{\frac{2\AllowableWindingTemperatureRise}{3 \ThermalResistance \PhaseResistance}} \] The factor \(2/3\) follows from the three-phase loss convention above; use the winding resistance at the limiting temperature. Other motor and inverter losses reduce the available thermal margin.

Transient torque peaks \(\PeakTorque \approx 2\text{--}4 \times \ContinuousTorque\) are permitted during aggressive dynamic maneuvers only if an active digital \({\MotorPhaseRmsCurrent}^2 \TimeInstant\) thermal protection observer running on the safety microcontroller bounds the thermal integral: \[ \JouleanTemperatureRise[\DiscreteSampleIndex+1] = \JouleanTemperatureRise[\DiscreteSampleIndex] e^{-\FiniteChange \TimeInstant / \ThermalTimeConstant} + \ThermalResistance \WindingLossPower[\DiscreteSampleIndex] \left( 1 - e^{-\FiniteChange \TimeInstant / \ThermalTimeConstant} \right) \] The thermal class is an insulation service rating, not a sharp instantaneous breakdown temperature. Current derating must begin with margin below the winding and rotor limits, accounting for model error, initial temperature, and response time.

Reflected rotor inertia and angular jerk limits

Geared electromagnetic actuators amplify delivered motor torque while multiplying rotor inertia at the joint output. For a transmission with gear reduction ratio \(\GearReductionRatio = \RotorAngularSpeed / \JointVelocity\), kinetic energy conservation dictates the reflected inertia acting at the output link: \[ \KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \RotorInertia \RotorAngularSpeed^2 + \frac{1}{2} \LoadInertia \JointVelocity^2 = \frac{1}{2} (\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia) \JointVelocity^2 \] The effective rotational inertia seen at joint coordinate \(\JointCoordinate\) is: \[ \EffectiveJointInertia = \LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia \] The joint equation of motion under motor electromagnetic torque \(\MotorTorque\) is: \[ \JointAcceleration = \frac{\GearReductionRatio \MotorTorque - \ExternalJointTorque}{\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia} \] Differentiating \(\JointAcceleration\) with respect to gear ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) at zero external load yields: \[ \frac{\partial \JointAcceleration}{\partial \GearReductionRatio} = \frac{\MotorTorque (\LoadInertia - \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia)}{(\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia)^2} \] Setting \(\frac{\partial \JointAcceleration}{\partial \GearReductionRatio} = 0\) yields the inertia-matching ratio \(\InertiaMatchingRatio = \sqrt{\LoadInertia / \RotorInertia}\). When \(\GearReductionRatio > \InertiaMatchingRatio\), counter-intuitively, increasing the gear ratio decreases delivered joint acceleration because the motor spends the majority of its torque accelerating its own rotor rather than the link.

Dynamic angular jerk and torque slew limits

For constant effective inertia and constant external torque, angular jerk \(\JointJerk(\TimeInstant) = \dddot{\JointCoordinate}(\TimeInstant)\) measures the time rate of change of joint acceleration: \[ \JointJerk(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\GearReductionRatio}{\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia} \frac{\DerivativeOperator\MotorTorque(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} \] Electromagnetic torque relates to stator quadrature current via torque constant \(\MotorTorqueConstant\): \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\). From the stator electrical dynamics in this section, current slew rate is bounded by winding inductance \(\PhaseInductance\): \[ \frac{\DerivativeOperator\MotorTorque}{d\TimeInstant} = \MotorTorqueConstant \frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} = \frac{\MotorTorqueConstant}{\PhaseInductance} \left( \WindingTerminalVoltage - \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed - \PhaseResistance \TorqueProducingCurrent \right) \] For a signed voltage constrained by \(|\WindingTerminalVoltage|\le \TerminalVoltageLimit\), a conservative magnitude bound in this scalar winding model is: \[ |\JointJerk| \le \frac{\GearReductionRatio \MotorTorqueConstant (\TerminalVoltageLimit + |\MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed + \PhaseResistance \TorqueProducingCurrent|)}{\PhaseInductance (\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia)} \] Positive and negative current slew have different available voltage headroom. A tighter directional bound retains the signs in the preceding current equation. Changing external torque, coupled joint motion, and inverter limits require additional terms.

When a tracker requests a step torque discontinuity \(\FiniteChange \JointTorque\) across discrete timesteps, the commanded derivative \(\frac{\DerivativeOperator\CommandedJointTorque}{d\TimeInstant} \to \infty\) cannot be delivered instantaneously by physical silicon. The inverter enters hard voltage saturation, causing steep current slew (\(d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)), intense thermal stress on power MOSFETs, and shock loads across gear-tooth contact flanks (\(\GearToothBendingStress \propto \GearTangentialLoad / (\GearFaceWidth \GearModule \LewisFormFactor)\)). Synchronous pre-actuation invariant filters evaluate numerical torque derivatives \((\JointTorque[\DiscreteSampleIndex] - \JointTorque[\DiscreteSampleIndex-1]) / \FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) to ensure requested actions remain within allowable jerk envelopes before latching power stages.

DC Bus Power Distribution and Energy Absorption

In data center machine learning clusters, electrical power is managed by high-capacity facility uninterruptible power supplies (UPS) and dedicated substation transformers. On mobile autonomous platforms, computational accelerators and mechanical actuators draw from a shared, finite DC power distribution network. A robotic platform’s electrical power distribution network must supply acceleration surges and absorb regenerative braking energy across finite bus capacitance and harness impedance.

Inductive droop and capacitive decoupling

When multiple high-torque actuators accelerate simultaneously (such as a humanoid jumping or an articulated arm bracing against an unexpected payload), total load current steps by \(\FiniteChange \LoadCurrent = \sum_{k=1}^{\ActuatorAxisCount} \FiniteChange \ConductorCurrent_k\) with steep current slew rates \(d\LoadCurrent/d\TimeInstant \sim 10^5\text{--}10^6\text{ A/s}\). The electrical wiring harness connecting the main battery to distributed joint inverters possesses parasitic loop inductance \(\PowerRailInductance\) (typically \(1\text{--}5\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{H}\) for a 1-meter harness) and harness resistance \(\PowerRailResistance\). These parasitics impede source current delivery, producing instantaneous inductive rail droop at the actuator inverter terminals: \[ \SourceVoltage(\TimeInstant)-\DcBusVoltage(\TimeInstant) = \PowerRailResistance \SourceCurrent(\TimeInstant) + \PowerRailInductance \frac{d\SourceCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} \] The capacitor is connected across this same bus node; its voltage change is not another independent series drop. For an ideal capacitor, \(\FiniteChange \BusCapacitorVoltage(\TimeInstant)=\DcBusVoltage(0)-\DcBusVoltage(\TimeInstant)\). Local inverter bus capacitance \(\DcBusBulkCapacitance\) must supply the instantaneous charge deficit between load demand and battery source current: \[ \FiniteChange \BusCapacitorVoltage(\TimeInstant) = \frac{1}{\DcBusBulkCapacitance} \int_0^\TimeInstant \left( \LoadCurrent(\IntegrationTimeInstant) - \SourceCurrent(\IntegrationTimeInstant) \right) d\IntegrationTimeInstant \]

If the DC bus terminal voltage drops below the drive’s configured Under-Voltage Lockout (UVLO) threshold \(\UvloThresholdVoltage\) (e.g., \(18\text{ V}\) on a \(24\text{ V}\) nominal bus, or \(36\text{ V}\) on a \(48\text{ V}\) bus), the gate driver IC enters UVLO fault protection. It disables gate bias within microseconds to prevent power MOSFETs from operating in their linear resistive region. The resulting loss of commanded torque can let a gravity-loaded joint descend. Current decay and the holding-brake response determine the plant motion.

If the current deficit starts at \(\LoadStepCurrentDeficit\) and decreases linearly to zero over \(\LoadStepDeficitDuration\), integrating that triangular deficit gives: \[ \DcBusBulkCapacitance \ge \frac{\LoadStepCurrentDeficit \cdot \LoadStepDeficitDuration}{2 \AllowableRailDroop} \] A constant deficit over the same interval removes the factor of two in the denominator. The source dynamics and capacitor ESR must also satisfy the terminal-voltage limit. Local capacitor banks combine low-ESR multi-layer ceramic capacitors (MLCCs) for high-frequency ripple filtering (\(20\text{--}100\text{ kHz}\) inverter switching) with bulk electrolytic or polymer capacitors for transient step energy buffering. The bank must also carry the RMS ripple current of the actual switching waveform. Equivalent-series-resistance losses scale with that current squared.

Regenerative braking surges and chopper sizing

During controlled regenerative deceleration or emergency stopping, mechanical kinetic energy \(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \BodySpeed^2\) (linear platform motion) or \(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveJointInertia \JointVelocity^2\) (articulated joint rotation) is converted back into electrical energy by the motor acting as a generator. Current flows back through the inverter bridge into the DC bus via active synchronous rectification or freewheeling body diodes.

If the battery cannot accept this regenerative current (for example, when the battery pack reaches its chemistry-specific charge limit, when battery internal resistance rises at freezing temperatures, or when safety contactors open to isolate the pack), the returned electrical energy \(\RegenerativeRecoveryEfficiency \KineticEnergy\) is forced into the local bus capacitor \(\DcBusBulkCapacitance\). The resulting terminal voltage surge follows the ideal capacitor relationship: \[ \FiniteChange \BusCapacitorEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \DcBusBulkCapacitance \left( \FinalBusVoltage^2 - \InitialBusVoltage^2 \right) = \RegenerativeRecoveryEfficiency \KineticEnergy \] \[ \FinalBusVoltage = \sqrt{ \InitialBusVoltage^2 + \frac{2 \RegenerativeRecoveryEfficiency \KineticEnergy}{\DcBusBulkCapacitance} } \]

An overvoltage protection trip and semiconductor breakdown are different thresholds. Exceeding the configured trip requests the assessed protective response; exceeding device voltage ratings can cause avalanche stress or damage. Disabling gate commands does not by itself absorb returned energy.

To prevent overvoltage destruction, motor drives incorporate an active dynamic brake chopper. When DC bus voltage crosses a hardware clamp threshold \(\ChopperTurnOnVoltage\), a high-power semiconductor switch (MOSFET or IGBT) modulates an auxiliary low-inductance braking resistor \(\BrakeChopperResistance\) with hysteresis:9 \[ \BrakeChopperResistance \le \frac{\ChopperClampVoltage^2}{\PeakRegenerativePower} \] \[ \PeakRegenerativePower \approx \RegenerativeRecoveryEfficiency \cdot \RegenerativeBrakingTorque \cdot \PeakRotorAngularSpeed \] The chopper comparator switches the resistor on when \(\DcBusVoltage \ge \ChopperTurnOnVoltage\) and turns off when \(\DcBusVoltage \le \ChopperTurnOffVoltage\), preventing rapid oscillation. In addition to peak power sizing, the braking resistor must be rated for single-pulse adiabatic energy absorption: \[ \BrakeResistorPulseEnergy \ge \RegenerativeRecoveryEfficiency \KineticEnergy \] with a thermal cooldown time constant that prevents cumulative thermal failure during repeated stop-and-go duty cycles.

9 Dynamic Braking Choppers: A qualified chopper can dissipate returned energy when the battery is unavailable as a sink. Its threshold, switch and resistor ratings, pulse duration, and thermal recovery must be tested. Regeneration and chopper operation are not inherent to every emergency stop; safe torque off can instead leave a motor coasting.

Real-Time Fieldbuses and Lock-Free Synchronization

In distributed machine learning clusters, networks are built on high-bandwidth fabrics (such as 100/400 Gbps RoCEv2 or InfiniBand) optimized for aggregate data-parallel throughput via credit-based flow control. In physical AI systems, however, the onboard communication fabric must simultaneously multiplex two diametrically opposed traffic classes:

  1. High-bandwidth best-effort perception streams: Multi-gigabit video from high-resolution GMSL2/GigE Vision cameras (\(2\text{--}8\text{ Gbps}\)) and 3D lidar point clouds (\(100\text{--}1000\text{ Mbps}\)) streaming into the AI accelerator’s unified memory for foundation model inference.
  2. Hard real-time periodic control traffic: Deterministic 1 kHz joint setpoint commands and high-resolution torque/position feedback (\(100\text{--}500\text{ kbps}\)) exchanged with distributed motor inverters.

If these two traffic classes share a naive best-effort Ethernet fabric with first-in, first-out (FIFO) queueing, multi-megabyte perception bursts cause severe queue congestion and buffer bloat. A burst of video frames can delay a motor control packet by \(5\text{--}20\text{ ms}\), violating the 1 ms control deadline, starving the inverter current loop, and tripping hardware emergency stops. Coordinating distributed drives while ingesting perception streams requires an architectural separation of fabrics, deterministic bus schedules, and bounded lock-free publication protocols.

Fieldbus serialization and schedulability

On shared physical buses, \(\ActuatorAxisCount\) actuator drives transmit telemetry and receive setpoints cyclically. To prevent perception traffic from corrupting motor timing, physical AI architectures use dedicated deterministic fieldbuses (CAN 2.0B, CAN FD, or EtherCAT) or time-scheduled switched Ethernet (IEEE TSN).

Classical Controller Area Network (CAN 2.0B, ISO 11898-1) uses differential signaling over a shielded twisted pair (\(\CanHighLineVoltage - \CanLowLineVoltage \approx 2.0\text{ V}\) for dominant logic 0, and \(0\text{ V}\) for recessive logic 1):

  • Arbitration Mechanics: Nodes access the physical medium using non-destructive bitwise arbitration. When two nodes transmit simultaneously, a dominant bit overrides a recessive bit. A node transmitting a recessive bit that samples a dominant bit on the physical bus immediately ceases transmission and yields the bus to the higher-priority frame without message corruption.
  • Framing and Payload Constraints: Standard CAN 2.0B frames support either an 11-bit standard or 29-bit extended identifier, with payload length strictly capped at \(\FramePayloadSize \le 8\text{ bytes}\) via a 4-bit Data Length Code (DLC).
  • Bit-Stuffing and Latency: Classical CAN inserts an opposite bit after a run of five identical bits in the stuffed region. Serialization time includes the identifier format, payload, CRC, stuffing, and interframe spacing. Let \(\FrameWireBitCount\) be that complete bit count for frame \(\FrameIndex\); then \(\FrameTransmissionTime_i=\FrameWireBitCount/\BusBitRate\). Extended identifiers consume more bits than standard identifiers.
  • The Scalability Bottleneck: The necessary load condition is \(\sum_i \FrameTransmissionTime_i/\FramePeriod_i\le1\), but meeting deadlines also requires priority and blocking analysis. There is no universal utilization threshold at which CAN queueing diverges. Axis capacity depends on command and feedback packing, periods, identifiers, and tolerated retransmissions; one frame per axis is a workload assumption.

Controller Area Network with Flexible Data-Rate (CAN FD, ISO 11898-1:2015) overcomes the payload and bandwidth limits of classical CAN through dual bit-rate switching and payload expansion up to 64 bytes:

  • Arbitration Phase: Transmitted at the nominal bit rate \(\NominalBitRate\) (typically \(500\text{ kbps}\) or \(1\text{ Mbps}\)). The bit time \(\NominalBitTime = 1/\NominalBitRate\) must accommodate physical cable propagation delays across the robot chassis and transceiver delays (\(2 \SignalPropagationDelay + \TransceiverDelay\)), allowing non-destructive bitwise priority arbitration. Nodes drive the bus using dominant or recessive states. If two nodes transmit simultaneously, the lower numerical message identifier (higher logical priority) overwrites recessive bits, winning the bus without collision or frame destruction.
  • Data Phase: Once arbitration is established, the winning node asserts the Bit Rate Switch (BRS) bit, accelerating transmission to the data bit rate \(\DataPhaseBitRate\) (typically \(2\text{--}8\text{ Mbps}\)). The data phase no longer needs round-trip arbitration within each bit. Signal integrity, receiver sampling, and transceiver delay compensation still constrain its rate.
  • Bit-Stuffing Overhead: Dynamic stuff bits are inserted after 5 consecutive identical bits in arbitration, while the CRC field incorporates fixed stuff bits with an explicit stuff count. In the worst-case bit pattern, bit stuffing can inflate frame transmission duration by up to \(20\text{ percent}\).

The serialization duration for frame \(\FrameIndex\) is: \[ \FrameSerializationTime \approx \frac{\ArbitrationFieldBitCount}{\NominalBitRate} + \frac{\DataFieldBitCount}{\DataPhaseBitRate} + \StuffBitTime + \InterframeSpacingTime \] where \(\ArbitrationFieldBitCount\) is the arbitration field length (identifier, control bits), \(\DataFieldBitCount\) is the payload and CRC length, \(\StuffBitTime\) accounts for stuff bits, and \(\InterframeSpacingTime\) is the minimum 3-bit inter-frame spacing.

In a CAN FD response-time budget, a higher-priority release cannot preempt a frame already transmitting. For a simplified error-free fixed-priority message set with no prior instance of frame \(\FrameIndex\) pending, the following conservative recurrence charges higher-priority interference across the whole response window: \[ \FrameResponseTime_i = \FrameTransmissionTime_i + \FrameBlockingTime_i + \sum_{j \in \HigherPriorityFrames(i)} \left\lceil \frac{\FrameResponseTime_i + \FrameReleaseJitter_j + \BitTime}{\FramePeriod_j} \right\rceil \FrameTransmissionTime_j \] Here \(\FrameTransmissionTime_i=\FrameSerializationTime\), \(\FrameReleaseJitter_j\) is higher-priority release jitter, and \(\BitTime\) accounts for arbitration at the boundary. Iterate from \(\FrameTransmissionTime_i+\FrameBlockingTime_i\) to convergence or a deadline overrun. Add the message’s own release jitter if its deadline starts before it becomes ready. The lower-priority blocking term is: \[ \FrameBlockingTime_i = \max_{k \in \LowerPriorityFrames(i)} \FrameTransmissionTime_k \] Set \(\FrameBlockingTime_i=0\) when no lower-priority frame exists. This simplified test is not an exact general CAN schedulability test: queued prior instances, controller transmit-buffer behavior, errors, and retransmissions require a matching busy-period analysis. A long CAN FD payload improves packing efficiency but can also increase the non-preemptive blocking seen by a short urgent command.

For high-axis-count systems (such as humanoids with 30 to 50 degrees of freedom), EtherCAT (ETG.1000) achieves deterministic 1 kHz to 10 kHz cycle times over standard 100BASE-TX full-duplex Ethernet (\(\LinkBitRate = 100\text{ Mbps}\)). Rather than transmitting individual frames per axis, EtherCAT structures communication as an active logical ring:

  1. The real-time master (executing on the safety microcontroller or PREEMPT_RT Linux host) transmits a single standard Ethernet frame (EtherType 0x88A4) traversing all slave drives in a physical daisy chain.
  2. Each drive integrates an EtherCAT Slave Controller (ESC) application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) (such as Beckhoff ET1100 or field-programmable gate array (FPGA) IP). As the frame’s bits pass through the ESC’s physical shift registers, the ESC processes data on the fly:
    • It reads commanded setpoints (joint target position, velocity, and feedforward torque) directly from its allocated byte offset in the datagram.
    • It writes actual measured feedback (encoder position, phase current, and drive status flags) into the moving frame.
  3. Each slave contributes a forwarding delay that depends on its controller and ports; the frame returns from the final slave along the return path. The example below assigns an illustrative delay per slave.

The cycle must accommodate the following round-trip budget for \(\SlaveDriveCount\) slave drives, with \(\FrameBitCount\) measured in bits and each delay bounded for the topology: \[ \FieldbusCycleTime \ge \frac{\FrameBitCount}{\LinkBitRate} + \MasterProcessingTime + \sum_{\SlaveDriveIndex=1}^\SlaveDriveCount \SlaveForwardingDelay + \SignalPropagationDelay + \CycleGuardTime \]

For 30 axes, the assumed command and feedback sizes give 600 bytes of process data. An illustrative 750 bytes frame takes 60 μs to serialize at 100 Mb/s. Charging 1 μs per slave adds 30 μs, so the subtotal is already 90 μs. Master processing, propagation, omitted wire overhead, and guard time must still fit the cycle. This partial sum is not a measured round-trip bound. EtherCAT distributed clocks align local device timers through measured offsets and propagation compensation. The qualified synchronization error and arrival deadline determine when a drive may use the frame. Clock synchronization does not remove frame transit time.

Time-sensitive networking and scheduled ethernet gates

While EtherCAT processes frames on the fly through specialized slave controllers, modern multi-camera and lidar robotic systems require multi-gigabit Ethernet fabrics that transport high-bandwidth vision data alongside hard real-time actuator commands. IEEE Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN) provides deterministic Ethernet via standards that bound queuing jitter and reserve transmission windows:

The IEEE 802.1Qbv Time-Aware Shaper (TAS) coordinates traffic transmission using hardware-controlled transmission gates associated with each priority egress queue (traffic classes 0 to 7). A cyclic Gate Control List (GCL), synchronized across all network bridges via IEEE 802.1AS (generalized Precision Time Protocol), switches gates open or closed at precise physical time epochs. By reserving dedicated time windows exclusively for high-priority Scheduled Traffic (ST), TAS eliminates queuing interference from lower-priority Best-Effort (BE) traffic.

Standard Ethernet frames are non-preemptive: once a transmission begins, it cannot be interrupted on physical copper or optical links. If a lower-priority best-effort packet begins transmission immediately before an ST time slice opens, the scheduled frame experiences transmission blocking of up to the maximum frame duration. To prevent this, TAS enforces a guard band before each scheduled traffic window: \[ \GuardBandDuration \ge \frac{\BestEffortWireBitCount}{\LinkBitRate} \] Here \(\BestEffortWireBitCount\) is the maximum non-preemptible wire occupancy in bits, including the applicable framing overhead. A payload MTU in bytes cannot be divided directly by a bit rate. The configured guard must also cover clock error and implementation timing uncertainty. During it, a best-effort frame may start only if it can finish before the reserved window.

IEEE 802.1Qbu and IEEE 802.3br frame preemption reduce blocking by interrupting eligible traffic at permitted fragment boundaries. The remaining bound includes the maximum non-preemptible fragment and its overhead, not merely a nominal minimum fragment size. Both the link partners and the schedule must support the mechanism. Preemption saves reserved idle time at the cost of fragmentation overhead and additional configuration obligations.

The four primary real-time communication protocols differ in framing, arbitration, clock synchronization, and axis-count scaling (table 7).

For either CAN variant, sum every command and feedback frame in the required cycle, then account for competing traffic, non-preemptive blocking, and the declared error model. EtherCAT avoids a separate frame for each axis, but its process-data size and forwarding delay still grow with the topology. TSN reserves service along a path. An end-to-end bound must include each hop’s schedule, serialization, forwarding, and synchronization uncertainty. These distinct budgets, rather than a protocol name or a universal axis count, support the selection in Real-Time Command Transport and Bus Determinism.

Lock-free seqlocks for multi-rate shared memory

When a producer updates multiword state read by a deadline-bound enforcer, waiting on a producer-held mutex can violate the reader’s deadline. The single-slot mailbox in algorithm 1 uses a sequence counter, which marks a single writer’s publication in progress with an odd value and a completed publication with an even value:

  1. Writer Protocol:
    • Publish an odd sequence value, write the payload, and make it visible through the platform’s coherent or cache-maintained memory contract.
    • Publish the next even value with release ordering.
  2. Reader Protocol:
    • Read \(\SequenceCounterReading_1\) with acquire ordering. If it is odd, reject this publication without waiting.
    • Make one bounded copy to scratch memory using the platform’s required load ordering, then read \(\SequenceCounterReading_2\).
    • Stage the candidate only if \(\SequenceCounterReading_1=\SequenceCounterReading_2\) and both are even, and the copied frame passes integrity, timestamp, and requested-expiry checks. Full admission remains separate. Retain the active command only while its own state, evidence, lease, and stopping margin permit; otherwise enter the state-matched fallback.

The algorithm is platform pseudocode, not portable C++ with a non-atomic payload: racing ordinary C++ loads and stores would be undefined behavior even if the sequence check later rejects the copy. The platform must define safe concurrent copying, cache maintenance, aligned counter atomicity, and wraparound behavior. On the machine’s discrete microcontroller, the writer is the link-receive interrupt and the reader is the permission tick on the same core, so the counter protocol needs only compiler ordering between them. A data memory barrier matters where a DMA engine or a second master writes the same memory. Arm M-profile cores provide that barrier only as the full-system DMB (ARM Limited 2012), so the OrderingFence steps below stand for whatever the target requires rather than an A-profile shareability option. The reader’s one-attempt execution time can be bounded on the target hardware; success probability depends on the writer’s schedule and cannot be inferred from payload size alone. Conventional Linux seqlock readers may retry, as the kernel documentation shows, but a real-time permission gate cannot use an unbounded retry loop.

ARM Limited. 2012. ARM Cortex-M Programming Guide to Memory Barrier Instructions. Application Note 321, ARM DAI 0321A. ARM Limited.
\(P\)

The proposal header and chunk payload of Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts cross the proposal boundary through this mailbox (Multi-Rate Cadences). Section 1.15.1 gives their byte layout. A successful read yields a candidate in owned staging memory. Admission separately checks producer sequence and lineage, the complete trajectory and stop, and the current seam state before activation (The Trajectory Record). Scheduling that work must preserve the permission tick; the scratch slot cannot be reused while admission still reads it. At activation the MCU rereads its clock, rechecks evidence and deadlines, and records the successful admission time. A mailbox read neither renews the active lease nor resets the evidence epoch.

Camera Projective Geometry and Covariance Propagation

In computer vision for machine learning, neural models typically process normalized pixel tensors to predict 2D bounding boxes or semantic segmentation masks. In physical AI systems, however, visual observations must be grounded as physical objects in continuous Euclidean 3D metric space to evaluate spatial clearance buffers \(\ClearDistance\) and enforce collision-avoidance invariants. Perception pipelines map continuous Euclidean 3D scenes onto discrete 2D camera photosite grids, and unproject pixel detections back into metric space under quantified measurement uncertainty.

Intrinsic projection matrix and radial distortion

Under the standard pinhole camera model, a 3D point \(\CameraFramePoint = [\CameraFrameHorizontalCoordinate, \CameraFrameVerticalCoordinate, \CameraFrameDepth]^\top\) in camera coordinates projects onto pixel coordinates \([\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate]^\top\) via the intrinsic calibration matrix \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix\):10 \[ \HomogeneousPixelCoordinates = \begin{bmatrix} \PixelColumnCoordinate \\ \PixelRowCoordinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{\CameraFrameDepth} \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix \CameraFramePoint = \frac{1}{\CameraFrameDepth} \begin{bmatrix} \HorizontalFocalLength & 0 & \PrincipalPointColumn \\ 0 & \VerticalFocalLength & \PrincipalPointRow \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \CameraFrameHorizontalCoordinate \\ \CameraFrameVerticalCoordinate \\ \CameraFrameDepth \end{bmatrix} \] where \((\HorizontalFocalLength, \VerticalFocalLength)\) are focal lengths in pixel units and \((\PrincipalPointColumn, \PrincipalPointRow)\) is the principal point. Non-linear radial lens distortion is modeled by distortion coefficients \((\RadialDistortionCoefficient_1, \RadialDistortionCoefficient_2)\): \[ \NormalizedRadialDistance^2 = \NormalizedImageAbscissa^2 + \NormalizedImageOrdinate^2, \quad \DistortedImageAbscissa = \NormalizedImageAbscissa (1 + \RadialDistortionCoefficient_1 \NormalizedRadialDistance^2 + \RadialDistortionCoefficient_2 \NormalizedRadialDistance^4), \quad \DistortedImageOrdinate = \NormalizedImageOrdinate (1 + \RadialDistortionCoefficient_1 \NormalizedRadialDistance^2 + \RadialDistortionCoefficient_2 \NormalizedRadialDistance^4) \] where \((\NormalizedImageAbscissa, \NormalizedImageOrdinate) = (\CameraFrameHorizontalCoordinate / \CameraFrameDepth, \CameraFrameVerticalCoordinate / \CameraFrameDepth)\) are normalized image coordinates.

10 Pinhole Intrinsic Projection: The pinhole model projects 3D points onto a 2D sensor through calibrated intrinsics. Under fixed pixel noise, the lateral variance after depth unprojection grows with squared distance; depth-error and calibration terms follow their own models. Uncalibrated intrinsics can bias a clearance estimate and must be included in its error envelope.

Metric unprojection and spatial covariance propagation

When unprojecting a 2D pixel observation \([\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate]^\top\) with measured depth \(\CameraFrameDepth\) into 3D camera coordinates, photosite measurement noise \(\MeasurementNoiseCovariance = \text{diag}(\PixelStandardDeviation^2, \PixelRowStandardDeviation^2, \DepthStandardDeviation^2)\) propagates through the unprojection Jacobian \(\UnprojectionJacobian\): \[ \CameraFramePoint = \begin{bmatrix} (\PixelColumnCoordinate - \PrincipalPointColumn) \CameraFrameDepth / \HorizontalFocalLength \\ (\PixelRowCoordinate - \PrincipalPointRow) \CameraFrameDepth / \VerticalFocalLength \\ \CameraFrameDepth \end{bmatrix}, \quad \UnprojectionJacobian = \frac{\partial \CameraFramePoint}{\partial [\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, \CameraFrameDepth]} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\CameraFrameDepth}{\HorizontalFocalLength} & 0 & \frac{\PixelColumnCoordinate - \PrincipalPointColumn}{\HorizontalFocalLength} \\ 0 & \frac{\CameraFrameDepth}{\VerticalFocalLength} & \frac{\PixelRowCoordinate - \PrincipalPointRow}{\VerticalFocalLength} \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \] The propagated camera-frame spatial covariance is: \[ \CameraFramePointCovariance = \UnprojectionJacobian \MeasurementNoiseCovariance \UnprojectionJacobian^\top \] Evaluating the diagonal terms shows that lateral positional variance along the camera \(\Glyph{X}\)-axis scales with the square of depth: \[ \CameraLateralStandardDeviation^2 \approx \left(\frac{\CameraFrameDepth}{\HorizontalFocalLength}\right)^2 \PixelStandardDeviation^2 + \left(\frac{\PixelColumnCoordinate - \PrincipalPointColumn}{\HorizontalFocalLength}\right)^2 \DepthStandardDeviation^2 \] For fixed pixel noise, the first variance contribution scales with \(\CameraFrameDepth^2\), so its standard deviation grows linearly with distance. The depth-error contribution follows the depth sensor’s own model. Transforming this 3D point into the robot body frame via rigid extrinsic transform \(\CameraExtrinsicTransform = [\CameraExtrinsicRotation, \CameraExtrinsicTranslation; \ZeroVector^\top, 1]\) yields the spatial body covariance: \[ \BodyFramePointCovariance = \CameraExtrinsicRotation \CameraFramePointCovariance \CameraExtrinsicRotation^\top + \ExtrinsicCalibrationCovariance \] where \(\ExtrinsicCalibrationCovariance\) models independent extrinsic uncertainty under this first-order approximation; correlations require the corresponding cross terms. A probabilistic clearance buffer may scale with \(\sqrt{\LargestEigenvalue(\BodyFramePointCovariance)}\) at a declared tail risk. A deterministic permission check instead needs a justified bounded-error envelope; covariance alone does not give a worst-case bound.

Zhang’s planar homography and camera calibration

Zhang’s calibration technique computes the camera intrinsic matrix \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix\) by observing a planar calibration rig (such as a checkerboard pattern) across \(\CalibrationViewCount \ge 3\) distinct orientations (Zhang 2000). Without loss of generality, assume the model plane lies on \(\ModelPointHeight = 0\) in world coordinates. A 3D model point \(\CalibrationModelPoint = [\ModelPointAbscissa, \ModelPointOrdinate, 0, 1]^\top\) maps to a 2D image point \(\HomogeneousPixelCoordinates = [\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, 1]^\top\) through a \(3 \times 3\) planar homography matrix \(\PlanarHomographyMatrix\): \[ \ProjectiveScaleFactor \begin{bmatrix} \PixelColumnCoordinate \\ \PixelRowCoordinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix \begin{bmatrix} \CameraRotationColumn_1 & \CameraRotationColumn_2 & \CameraRotationColumn_3 & \RigidTransformTranslation \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \ModelPointAbscissa \\ \ModelPointOrdinate \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix \begin{bmatrix} \CameraRotationColumn_1 & \CameraRotationColumn_2 & \RigidTransformTranslation \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \ModelPointAbscissa \\ \ModelPointOrdinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \PlanarHomographyMatrix \begin{bmatrix} \ModelPointAbscissa \\ \ModelPointOrdinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} \] where \(\PlanarHomographyMatrix = [\HomographyColumn_1 \; \HomographyColumn_2 \; \HomographyColumn_3] = \HomographyScaleFactor \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix [\CameraRotationColumn_1 \; \CameraRotationColumn_2 \; \RigidTransformTranslation]\) with arbitrary nonzero scalar \(\HomographyScaleFactor\).

Zhang, Zhengyou. 2000. “A Flexible New Technique for Camera Calibration.” IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence 22 (11): 1330–34. https://doi.org/10.1109/34.888718.

Orthogonality constraints on the absolute conic

Because the rotation vectors \(\CameraRotationColumn_1\) and \(\CameraRotationColumn_2\) are orthonormal columns of an \(\SpecialOrthogonalGroup\) matrix (\(\CameraRotationColumn_1^\top \CameraRotationColumn_2 = 0\) and \(\|\CameraRotationColumn_1\| = \|\CameraRotationColumn_2\| = 1\)), inverting \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix\) yields: \[ \CameraRotationColumn_1 = \frac{1}{\HomographyScaleFactor} \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \HomographyColumn_1, \quad \CameraRotationColumn_2 = \frac{1}{\HomographyScaleFactor} \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \HomographyColumn_2 \] Imposing orthonormality on \(\CameraRotationColumn_1\) and \(\CameraRotationColumn_2\) defines two algebraic constraints per homography: \[ \HomographyColumn_1^\top \CameraIntrinsicInverseTranspose \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \HomographyColumn_2 = 0 \] \[ \HomographyColumn_1^\top \CameraIntrinsicInverseTranspose \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \HomographyColumn_1 - \HomographyColumn_2^\top \CameraIntrinsicInverseTranspose \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \HomographyColumn_2 = 0 \] The symmetric matrix \(\AbsoluteConicImageMatrix = \CameraIntrinsicInverseTranspose \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1}\) represents the image of the absolute conic (IAC): \[ \AbsoluteConicImageMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{11} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{12} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{13} \\ \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{12} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{22} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{23} \\ \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{13} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{23} & \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{33} \end{bmatrix} \] Defining the 6-vector \(\AbsoluteConicVector = [\AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{11}, \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{12}, \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{22}, \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{13}, \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{23}, \AbsoluteConicImageEntry_{33}]^\top\), each quadratic term \(\HomographyColumn_i^\top \AbsoluteConicImageMatrix \HomographyColumn_j\) can be rewritten as the linear inner product \(\HomographyConstraintVector_{ij}^\top \AbsoluteConicVector\), where: \[ \HomographyConstraintVector_{ij} = \begin{bmatrix} \HomographyColumnEntry_{i1}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j1} \\ \HomographyColumnEntry_{i1}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j2} + \HomographyColumnEntry_{i2}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j1} \\ \HomographyColumnEntry_{i2}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j2} \\ \HomographyColumnEntry_{i3}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j1} + \HomographyColumnEntry_{i1}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j3} \\ \HomographyColumnEntry_{i3}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j2} + \HomographyColumnEntry_{i2}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j3} \\ \HomographyColumnEntry_{i3}\HomographyColumnEntry_{j3} \end{bmatrix} \] The two constraints per observed orientation become the linear system: \[ \begin{bmatrix} \HomographyConstraintVector_{12}^\top \\ (\HomographyConstraintVector_{11} - \HomographyConstraintVector_{22})^\top \end{bmatrix} \AbsoluteConicVector = \ZeroVector \] Stacking \(\CalibrationViewCount \ge 3\) images forms the homogeneous system \(\CalibrationConstraintMatrix \AbsoluteConicVector = \ZeroVector\), where \(\CalibrationConstraintMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{2\CalibrationViewCount \times 6}\). The right singular vector of \(\CalibrationConstraintMatrix\) corresponding to the smallest singular value provides \(\AbsoluteConicVector\) up to a scale factor. The intrinsic parameters \((\HorizontalFocalLength, \VerticalFocalLength, \PrincipalPointColumn, \PrincipalPointRow)\) and skew \(\CameraSkewCoefficient\) are then extracted, after fixing the scale and sign, via a positive-definite factorization of \(\AbsoluteConicImageMatrix\). Degenerate views or measurement noise can make that factorization invalid. Distinct orientations alone do not guarantee a well-conditioned calibration. This closed-form linear solution initializes the non-linear Levenberg–Marquardt optimization minimizing reprojection residuals over all observed fiducial corners.

Kinematic Stopping Envelopes and Information Age Lag

In distributed systems, pipeline latency represents elapsed waiting time or queuing delay. In physical AI, end-to-end software latency converts directly into irreversible physical displacement, uninspected “blind travel,” during which an embodied machine traverses space with undiminished momentum before actuators can begin retarding motion. Halting an embodied machine transporting mass \(\Mass\) at velocity \(\SpeedAtDetection\) requires balancing sensorimotor information freshness, neural compute budgets, and fieldbus dispatch against physical mechanical braking deceleration.

Sensorimotor information age and lag distance

For a stop triggered by a specified hazard detector and stop path, let \(\PrebrakeDelay\) bound the time from the physical state represented by the evidence until validated braking deceleration begins (Watchdog validity leases and teleoperation latency bounds gives the lease-expiry form, which charges the lease interval in place of recognition and compute): \[ \PrebrakeDelay = \ObservationAge + \DetectionInterval + \StopDecisionComputeBound + \CommandTransportTime + \ActuatorResponseTime \] The five intervals partition the pre-brake timeline without overlap:

  • \(\ObservationAge\) is the upper evidence age at a declared decision-start epoch, including capture/readout, completed processing and transport, and clock-conversion uncertainty.
  • \(\DetectionInterval\) bounds any remaining recognition or expiry-detection interval after that epoch; work already included in evidence age is not charged twice.
  • \(\StopDecisionComputeBound\) bounds subsequent stop-decision work and any still-required input transfer.
  • \(\CommandTransportTime\) bounds command dispatch and network queuing.
  • \(\ActuatorResponseTime\) ends when the measured actuator begins delivering the specified deceleration, not when a command register is written.

The intervals must be disjoint and ordered along the actual stop path. Clearance and motion must share a declared epoch: if the clearance is already propagated to current time, do not subtract historical travel a second time. Under the explicit simplifying assumption that speed cannot increase before brake onset, the blind travel is bounded by \(\BlindTravelDistance=\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\). If propulsion or a slope can increase speed, use its validated acceleration bound in this interval instead.

Braking dynamics and friction limits

Tire-floor friction and actuator torque place upper limits on achievable deceleration. The bound \(\AchievedBrakingDeceleration\le\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity\) does not establish what braking the machine will deliver. Let \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration>0\) be a validated lower bound on achieved deceleration throughout the stop, for the declared load, temperature, slope, brake condition, and surface. A justified friction floor \(\FrictionCoefficientFloor\) may support that bound, but does not replace brake tests. With constant or stronger braking after onset, a localization bound \(\LocalizationBound\), a fixed protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\), and a tracking-error bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\), \[ \StoppingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)=\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay+\frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}+\LocalizationBound+\ProtectiveClearanceMargin+\TrackingErrorBound. \] Motion is admitted only when \(\StoppingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\le \ClearDistance\), where \(\ClearDistance\) is taken as a validated lower bound on available path clearance. This is a constant-deceleration reference model. The smooth stop in Stopping Envelopes ramps acceleration from and back to zero, so it does not satisfy a strictly positive deceleration floor throughout braking. For that profile, replace \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\) with the integral of its validated speed trajectory and use its full duration. The sampled controller must reserve intersample travel and a state-matched stop before the viable region is lost.

Kinetic energy derating and friction collapse

In an ideal friction-limited model, lowering \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) from \(\NominalFrictionCoefficient\) to a positive \(\DegradedFrictionCoefficient\) changes the braking-distance ratio to \(\NominalFrictionCoefficient/\DegradedFrictionCoefficient\). That ratio is illustrative; actual deceleration can also be limited by brake hardware or control. If surface friction is not sensed in time, derive \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) from a controlled surface condition and inspection schedule, exclude degraded surfaces, or withhold motion. For the constant-speed pre-brake model above, solving the clearance inequality gives \[ \ClearanceSpeedClamp=-\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\PrebrakeDelay+\sqrt{(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\PrebrakeDelay)^2+2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\left(\ClearDistance-\LocalizationBound-\ProtectiveClearanceMargin-\TrackingErrorBound\right)}. \] This clamp is valid only when \(\ClearDistance\) exceeds the three fixed allowances, the braking lower bound and timing upper bounds hold, and the speed bound is enforced before entering the hazard corridor. No positive clamp follows if the available deceleration floor is unknown or zero.

Machine Data Sheet and Record Register

Every calculation on the running machine draws its inputs from one registry entry, the warehouse mobile manipulator at its aisle site. This section collects those inputs in one place, so a chapter can cite a value by name rather than restate it. It also collects the records the chapters produce. The machine card of The running machine prints the parameters used most often; the data sheet below lists them all.

The machine data sheet

Each running-machine input carries its evidence category in the reader guide’s vocabulary (table 8). A derived value inherits the weakest category among its inputs, so most derived rows remain illustrative evidence even though they are computed exactly. A row marked needs source names the measurement that would supply it; until that measurement exists, any claim that depends on the row is open, as the tip-over check on the credible deceleration is. The safety laser scanner carries the certified stop for a person at the rack end, while the perception LiDAR feeds only the permission path’s unrated admission checks above it. The safety scanner’s rows describe a representative certified unit, not a qualified selection, and the stopping budgets in this book are the permission path’s, not the scanner’s. A certified protective field is sized with the scanner’s own response time under ISO 13855 (International Organization for Standardization 2024).

International Organization for Standardization. 2024. ISO 13855:2024: Safety of Machinery, Positioning of Safeguards with Respect to the Approach of the Human Body. International Organization for Standardization.

The record register

A record is a machine-checkable statement of an assumption together with its evidence, its producer, its consumers, and the conditions that invalidate it. Sixteen records form the record chain of The spine of the book, one from each chapter of Parts I to IV except The Cognitive Brain, whose proposer limits enter the handoff record, and one from Engineering Physical AI. Table 9 gives each record one name. Two names shorten after first mention and in figure art, the provenance record and the belief record. Names in the last column are retired; where one survives in figure art, it names the same record. The wire formats in section 1.15 lay out the records that cross a mailbox or are signed offline.

The chain has two strands. Records 1 through 5 state what the plant can do and what the proposer has shown it can do; records 6 through 9 carry what the proposer proposes now. They meet in the enforcement record. The placement record then tests whether the permission path keeps its deadline on the chosen silicon, and records 12 through 16 carry the result into governance.

Wire Formats for the Record Chain

A mailbox decoder needs a bounded amount of work and an unambiguous representation. The layouts below specify byte offsets, field lengths, and validation rules across the proposal boundary between application processors and the independent enforcer. Fixed headers and bounded arrays make resource requirements explicit. The chosen serialization framework alone does not determine allocation or timing behavior.

  1. Zero Dynamic Allocation Invariant: Deserialization must never invoke dynamic memory managers (malloc, free, C++ new, delete). Dynamic heap allocators manage internal free-list bins, take global allocator mutexes, and suffer from heap fragmentation. Under memory pressure, heap operations can invoke the brk() or mmap() system calls, triggering kernel memory compaction or page faults that stall execution for tens of milliseconds. In hard real-time control, all record slots, mailbox staging areas, scratch buffers, and circular telemetry logs must be statically sized at compile time and preallocated in dedicated static SRAM or Data Tightly Coupled Memory (DTCM) pools.
  2. Bounded Parse and Validation Work: Header fields have fixed offsets; arrays have validated counts and configured maxima. Reading one scalar is constant-time, but copying, hashing, and validating a complete record scale with its bounded length. Full trajectory admission remains interruptible and separate from the per-tick check.
  3. Natural Alignment Across Heterogeneous ABIs: On heterogeneous SoCs combining 64-bit application processors (ARM Cortex-A or x86_64) and 32-bit real-time microcontrollers (ARM Cortex-R52 or Cortex-M7), data types must be naturally aligned. A scalar of size \(\ScalarFieldSize\) bytes must reside at an offset satisfying: \[ \text{offset} \equiv 0 \pmod \ScalarFieldSize \] Specifically, 64-bit timestamps and integers (uint64_t, float64) align on 8-byte boundaries (\(\ScalarFieldSize=8\)), 32-bit values (uint32_t, float32) align on 4-byte boundaries (\(\ScalarFieldSize=4\)), and 16-bit integers (uint16_t) align on 2-byte boundaries (\(\ScalarFieldSize=2\)). Explicit padding bytes between narrower fields maintain these alignment invariants across both 32-bit and 64-bit compilers.

An alignment violation has direct microarchitectural consequences: a core may trap an unsupported unaligned access or split it into multiple bus operations. The result depends on instruction width, memory attributes, and processor configuration. A safety integrity classification does not itself specify those semantics. Explicit reserved bytes, scalar encodings, endianness, and schema versions define the wire representation. A native C structure is not that representation by default. Use checked offsets or explicit serialization and verify layout on each ABI. Alignment alone also does not guarantee atomicity of a wide access on a narrower core.

Not every record in table 9 needs a byte layout. The layouts below cover the proposal header that every proposal carries and the records that cross a mailbox or are signed offline. Runtime proposals and evidence use bounded records where they cross mailboxes or fieldbuses, while limits, evaluation, enforcement configuration, placement evidence, and release artifacts are retained under canonical encodings and linked by digest. All records use standard little-endian byte ordering for multi-byte scalars (uint64_t, uint32_t, uint16_t, float32, float64). Lineage is established by embedding parent SHA-256 digests. Runtime corruption is caught via IEEE 802.3 CRC-32 checksums over headers and payloads. Operator authority is authenticated via AES-128 CMAC tags and Ed25519 digital signatures. The authority log is chained with HMAC-SHA-256. Offline records (placement, fault, release, approval) are identified by the SHA-256 digest of their canonical encoding, and the record that consumes one stores that digest under the consumed record’s name.

Proposal header and chunk payload

Every proposal that crosses the proposal boundary opens with the same header, and payload_kind selects the payload that follows at 0x50. Table 10 lays out the header with the minimal chunk payload of Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts. The chunk carries kinematic setpoints only. Torque is computed on the permission side, where the tracker derives it from the admitted reference, so a learned proposal never names a motor torque.

Intent payload

The intent lease of The Intent Lease Specification and Contract travels under the proposal header with payload_kind set to INTENT (table 11). Three header fields take specific meanings. t_evidence_ns is the parent belief record’s evidence epoch, never its estimate epoch. parent_digest is that belief record’s digest, which the permission path matches against a retained record. t_expire_ns is the requested expiry, which the permission path shortens to the earliest of the evidence horizon, its configured lease ceiling, and the stop deadline. The six bounds values are three conservative position-error envelopes and three task tolerances in the base frame named by frame_id. They are not an encoding of a covariance, and the producer must justify any conversion from its covariance estimate, including calibration bias and the declared tail risk.

Trajectory payload

The trajectory record of The Trajectory Record replaces the chunk on the running machine and travels under the same proposal header, with payload_kind set to TRAJECTORY and parent_digest set to the digest of the active intent lease (table 12). The header’s t_expire_ns requests an absolute expiry. The MCU retains an admitted expiry no later than that request or the successful admission time plus the configured chunk lease. Earlier evidence and stop deadlines can shorten it further. The parent intent’s expiry comes from the lease that parent_digest identifies. The MCU records admission time locally, without changing t_evidence_ns or t_issue_ns. The layout below illustrates a single polynomial stopping suffix per joint. It does not encode the piecewise S-curve worked in The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking. That profile requires its own specified bounded encoding and decoder, with decoding and validation charged to admission. The setpoint samples and the assumptions sidecar travel outside this fixed part. The permission path verifies sidecar_digest over both, validates the complete candidate in staging memory, and latches the admitted setpoints and stop suffix in protected memory before tracking begins.

Enforcement record

The enforcement record of The Enforcement Record is configuration, not a proposal, so it carries no proposal header (table 13). Three digests name what the record rests on: the evaluation record, the limit records behind the stopping envelope, and the validity region. Each threshold names the fallback-ladder rung that a failed check selects. Where the rung depends on whether the body is moving, the row names both, STOP in motion and HOLD at standstill. Refusing a replacement selects no rung if the active record still passes its own state, evidence, lease, and stopping checks. Failure of an active-record check selects the applicable fallback.

The operating evidence ceiling must charge capture-to-admission age and permitted tracking time while reserving the response and stopping distance derived in Safe Sets as Conditional Permission. The perception dispatch-age gate applies at dispatch only. It cannot serve as this operating ceiling. The dispatch-to-admission interval remains a separate qualification obligation, and the partial stopping budget cannot establish the ceiling without that bound and the required reserve. Each runtime check uses the current MCU time and the retained evidence epoch; admission never makes old evidence fresh.

Placement record

The placement record of The Placement Record is offline evidence with a fixed header and a linked test report (table 14). It carries no signature of its own; the signed release manifest binds it through placement_record_digest. enforcement_record_digest names the enforcement record whose deadline_ns was tested. test_report_digest identifies the external test report. That report states the declared worst-case execution time the loaded test must not exceed, together with distributions, sample counts, test conditions, mitigations, and untested states. A maximum that has not been measured is stored as all ones, never zero, the same unavailable-value convention the fault record uses.

Authority record and operator payload

The authority record of The Authority Record has two runtime layouts. A network operator’s command is a proposal-header payload (table 15). It is the one proposal whose sender’s identity confers authority, so it carries an AES-128 CMAC tag under the operator role’s key, the primitive whose per-frame cost Authenticating an Intervention prices. The static authority manifest fixes each role’s channel grant, the command’s component count, order, and units, and the key custody.

The authority log (table 16), the per-tick part of the authority record, is written once per tick, not sent as a packet.
